Present Simple

Learning goal: Describe habits, facts, and routines

What is present simple?

Present simple is the most common tense in English. You use it for:

  1. Habits and routines — things you do regularly
  2. Facts — things that are always true
  3. Permanent situations — things that don't change often

Key pattern: Subject + base verb (+ s for he/she/it)

How to form present simple

Positive sentences

SubjectVerbExample
IstudyI study English every day.
youstudyYou study at home.
he / she / itstudiesShe studies in the morning.
westudyWe study together.
theystudyThey study online.

Rule: Add -s or -es for he/she/it only.

When to add -s, -es, or -ies

Verb endingRuleExamples
Most verbsAdd -swork → works, eat → eats, read → reads
-ch, -sh, -ss, -x, -oAdd -esteach → teaches, wash → washes, go → goes
Consonant + yChange y to -iesstudy → studies, try → tries, carry → carries
Vowel + yAdd -splay → plays, say → says, buy → buys

Special verbs:

  • have → has (not haves)
  • do → does (not dos)

Dari tip: Dari verbs don't change based on who is doing the action (من می‌خوانم / او می‌خواند both use "می‌خواند"). English adds -s for he/she/it — practice this daily.

Uses of present simple

1. Habits and routines

Things you do regularly (every day, often, sometimes, never).

  • I wake up at 6 AM.
  • She drinks tea every morning.
  • We study English three times a week.

Common time expressions:

  • every day / week / month / year
  • always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never
  • on Mondays, in the morning, at night

2. Facts and general truths

Things that are always true.

  • The sun rises in the east.
  • Water boils at 100°C.
  • English has 26 letters.
  • Kabul is in Afghanistan.

3. Permanent situations

Things that are true for a long time.

  • I live in Afghanistan.
  • She works from home.
  • They speak Dari and Hazaragi.

Negative form: use "do not" / "does not"

SubjectNegativeShort formExample
I / you / we / theydo not + base verbdon'tI do not study at night. / I don't study at night.
he / she / itdoes not + base verbdoesn'tShe does not work on Friday. / She doesn't work on Friday.

Important rules:

  • Use the base form of the verb after don't/doesn't (no -s)
  • ✅ She doesn't study. (base form)
  • ❌ She doesn't studies. (wrong — no -s after doesn't)

Examples:

  • I don't drink coffee.
  • He doesn't have a computer.
  • We don't go to school.
  • She doesn't like noise.

Question form: use "do" / "does"

Yes/no questions

SubjectQuestion formAnswer
I / you / we / theyDo + subject + base verb?Yes, I do. / No, I don't.
he / she / itDoes + subject + base verb?Yes, she does. / No, she doesn't.

Examples:

  • Do you study English? → Yes, I do. / No, I don't.
  • Does she work at home? → Yes, she does. / No, she doesn't.
  • Do they speak English? → Yes, they do. / No, they don't.

Again: Use base form after do/does (no -s).

Wh- questions

Put the question word first, then do/does:

Question wordStructureExample
WhatWhat + do/does + subject + verb?What do you study?
WhereWhere + do/does + subject + verb?Where does she live?
WhenWhen + do/does + subject + verb?When do they work?
WhyWhy + do/does + subject + verb?Why do you learn English?
HowHow + do/does + subject + verb?How do you study?
WhoWho + does + subject + verb?Who does she help?

Note: When "who" is the subject, don't use do/does:

  • Who studies English? → Sara studies English. (no "do")

Time expressions with present simple

ExpressionPositionExample
every day / week / monthEnd of sentenceI study every day.
always, usually, oftenBefore main verbI always wake up early.
sometimesBefore main verb or startI sometimes drink tea. / Sometimes I drink tea.
neverBefore main verbI never skip practice.
in the morning / eveningEnd of sentenceShe works in the morning.
on Monday / FridayEnd of sentenceWe have class on Monday.

Hazaragi tip: Word order for time expressions may feel different. Practice placing "always/often/sometimes" BEFORE the main verb: "I always study," not "I study always."

Practice activities

Activity 1: Add -s or -es

Convert these to third person (he/she/it):

  1. I work → She ___
  2. You teach → He ___
  3. We go → It ___
  4. They study → She ___
  5. I watch → He ___

Answers: 1. works | 2. teaches | 3. goes | 4. studies | 5. watches

Activity 2: Make negative sentences

  1. I study at night. → I don't study at night.
  2. She has Wi-Fi. → She doesn't have Wi-Fi.
  3. They work on Friday. → They don't work on Friday.

Activity 3: Make questions

  1. You speak English. → Do you speak English?
  2. She lives in Kabul. → Does she live in Kabul?
  3. They study online. → Do they study online?

Activity 4: Write your routine

Describe your daily routine using 8 present simple sentences.

Example:

  • I wake up at 6:30.
  • I have tea and bread.
  • I study English for 30 minutes.
  • My sister works from home.
  • We eat lunch together.
  • I read in the afternoon.
  • I don't go out at night.
  • I sleep at 10 PM.

Self-check quiz

  1. Add the right form: She ___ (study) English every day. (studies)
  2. Make negative: He works at home. → He ___ at home. (doesn't work)
  3. Make question: They speak Dari. → ___ they ___ Dari? (Do / speak)
  4. Fix this error: "She don't like tea."
  5. Where do you put "always" in this sentence: "I am tired"?

Answers: 1. studies | 2. doesn't work | 3. Do / speak | 4. She doesn't like tea. | 5. I am always tired. (after "be")

Common mistakes

MistakeWhy wrongCorrect
She study English.Missing -sShe studies English.
He don't work.Wrong formHe doesn't work.
Does she works?Extra -s after "does"Does she work?
I always am tired.Wrong positionI am always tired.
Do you can help me?Mixed with modalCan you help me?

Present simple vs present continuous

Many learners confuse these two. Here's the difference:

Present SimplePresent Continuous
Habits, routines, factsHappening right now
I study every day.I am studying right now.
She works at home.She is working at the moment.

If it happens regularly → present simple
If it's happening at this exact moment → present continuous (covered in Intermediate Growth)

Daily practice (10 minutes)

Day 1: Write 10 sentences about your habits (I study, I eat, I sleep…)
Day 2: Write 10 sentences about someone else (She works, He reads…)
Day 3: Make 5 negative sentences
Day 4: Make 5 yes/no questions and answer them
Day 5: Make 5 wh- questions
Day 6: Write 1 paragraph (8-10 sentences) about your daily routine
Day 7: Review all mistakes and rewrite correctly

Next steps

Once you master present simple:

  1. Learn Questions & Negatives — deeper practice
  2. Study Articles (a, an, the)
  3. Move to Intermediate Growth for past and future tenses
  4. Practice with Grammar Drills

Related pages:

  • Core Grammar — full chapter
  • Be & Have
  • Questions & Negatives
  • Dari companion

Questions & Negatives

Learning goal: Ask clear questions and make negative statements correctly

Why questions and negatives matter

Being able to ask questions lets you:

  • Get information you need
  • Clarify when you don't understand
  • Have real conversations

Negative sentences let you:

  • Say what you don't do or don't have
  • Correct mistakes
  • Express preferences

Both are essential for communication beyond basic statements.

Yes/No questions (expect "yes" or "no" answer)

With "be" (am, is, are)

Pattern: Move "be" to the front

StatementQuestionAnswer
You are ready.Are you ready?Yes, I am. / No, I'm not.
She is at home.Is she at home?Yes, she is. / No, she isn't.
They are students.Are they students?Yes, they are. / No, they aren't.

With "have" (possession, not "have to")

Pattern: Use Do / Does + subject + have

StatementQuestionAnswer
You have a phone.Do you have a phone?Yes, I do. / No, I don't.
She has a sister.Does she have a sister?Yes, she does. / No, she doesn't.

Important: Don't say "Have you a phone?" in American English. Use "Do you have a phone?"

With other verbs (present simple)

Pattern: Use Do / Does + subject + base verb

StatementQuestionAnswer
You study English.Do you study English?Yes, I do. / No, I don't.
She works at home.Does she work at home?Yes, she does. / No, she doesn't.
They speak Dari.Do they speak Dari?Yes, they do. / No, they don't.

Remember: After "does," use the base form (no -s).

  • ✅ Does she work?
  • ❌ Does she works?

Dari tip: In Dari, you can make a question just by changing your tone. In English, you MUST change the word order: "Do you study?" not "You study?"

Wh- questions (need specific information)

Question words

WordAsks aboutExample
Whatthing or actionWhat do you study?
WhereplaceWhere do you live?
WhentimeWhen do you study?
WhopersonWho is your teacher?
WhyreasonWhy do you learn English?
Howmanner or methodHow do you study?
WhichchoiceWhich book do you like?
WhosepossessionWhose phone is this?
How manyquantity (countable)How many sisters do you have?
How muchquantity (uncountable) or priceHow much time do you have?

Pattern for wh- questions

With "be":

  • Question word + be + subject
  • Where are you?
  • Who is she?
  • Why is he late?

With other verbs:

  • Question word + do/does + subject + base verb
  • What do you study?
  • Where does she work?
  • When do they eat lunch?

Special case: "Who" as subject

When "who" is doing the action (subject), don't use do/does:

  • Who studies English? → Sara studies English.
  • Who lives here? → My family lives here.
  • Who has a phone? → She has a phone.

But when "who" is the object:

  • Who do you help? → I help my sister.
  • Who does she call? → She calls her friend.

"How" questions with adjectives

PatternExampleAsks about
How oldHow old are you?Age
How tallHow tall is he?Height
How longHow long is the class?Duration
How farHow far is Kabul?Distance
How oftenHow often do you study?Frequency

Hazaragi tip: "How" questions in Hazaragi might use different structures. In English, put "How + adjective" at the start: "How old are you?" not "You are how old?"

Negative sentences

With "be"

Pattern: Subject + be + not

StatementNegativeContraction
I am tired.I am not tired.I**'m not** tired.
You are late.You are not late.You aren't late.
She is here.She is not here.She isn't here.
We are ready.We are not ready.We aren't ready.

With "have"

Pattern: Subject + do/does not have

StatementNegativeContraction
I have time.I do not have time.I don't have time.
She has Wi-Fi.She does not have Wi-Fi.She doesn't have Wi-Fi.
They have books.They do not have books.They don't have books.

With other verbs

Pattern: Subject + do/does not + base verb

StatementNegativeContraction
I study at night.I do not study at night.I don't study at night.
She works on Friday.She does not work on Friday.She doesn't work on Friday.
They speak English.They do not speak English.They don't speak English.

Critical rule: After "do not" / "does not," use the base form (no -s).

  • ✅ She doesn't work.
  • ❌ She doesn't works.

Never, rarely, seldom (negative meaning)

These words have negative meaning, so don't use "not" with them:

CorrectWrong
I never study at night.I don't never study. ❌
She rarely calls.She doesn't rarely call. ❌
We seldom go out.We don't seldom go. ❌

Short answers

When someone asks a yes/no question, answer with yes/no + subject + auxiliary.

With "be"

  • Are you tired? → Yes, I am. / No, I'm not.
  • Is she home? → Yes, she is. / No, she isn't.

With "do/does"

  • Do you study? → Yes, I do. / No, I don't.
  • Does she work? → Yes, she does. / No, she doesn't.

Don't repeat the main verb in short answers:

  • ❌ Do you study? → Yes, I study.
  • ✅ Do you study? → Yes, I do.

Question tags (confirmation)

A question tag turns a statement into a question to confirm information.

Pattern: Positive statement → negative tag | Negative statement → positive tag

StatementTagFull sentence
You are ready,aren't you?You are ready, aren't you?
She works here,doesn't she?She works here, doesn't she?
They don't know,do they?They don't know, do they?

Use: Check if something is true, or invite agreement.

Practice activities

Activity 1: Make yes/no questions

  1. You are tired. → Are you tired?
  2. She has a phone. → Does she have a phone?
  3. They study English. → Do they study English?

Activity 2: Make wh- questions

Ask about the bold part:

  1. I live in Kabul. → Where do you live?
  2. She studies in the morning. → When does she study?
  3. I study English. → What do you study?

Activity 3: Make negative

  1. I am busy. → I am not busy.
  2. She works at night. → She doesn't work at night.
  3. They have Wi-Fi. → They don't have Wi-Fi.

Activity 4: Short answers

  1. Do you study English? → Yes, I do.
  2. Is she at home? → No, she isn't.
  3. Does he work? → Yes, he does.

Self-check quiz

  1. Make question: "She is tired." → ___?
  2. Make question: "You study English." → ___?
  3. Ask about time: "I study at night." → ___?
  4. Make negative: "I have time." → ___
  5. Fix error: "Where you live?"

Answers:

  1. Is she tired?
  2. Do you study English?
  3. When do you study?
  4. I don't have time. / I do not have time.
  5. Where do you live?

Common mistakes

MistakeWhy wrongCorrect
You are tired? (with rising tone only)Need word order change in writingAre you tired?
Do you can help?Mixed auxiliaryCan you help?
She doesn't works.Extra -s after doesn'tShe doesn't work.
Where you live?Missing "do"Where do you live?
I no have time.Wrong negative formI don't have time.
Do you study? Yes, I study.Repeating verbYes, I do.

Polite questions

Add please to make requests more polite:

  • Can you help me, please?
  • Please, can you speak slowly?
  • Do you have time, please?

Use Could instead of Can for extra politeness (covered in intermediate):

  • Could you help me, please? (more polite)

Daily practice plan

Day 1: Write 10 yes/no questions with "be"
Day 2: Write 10 yes/no questions with "do/does"
Day 3: Write 10 wh- questions (mix: what, where, when, why, how)
Day 4: Write 10 negative sentences
Day 5: Practice short answers (write Q&A pairs)
Day 6: Write 5 question tags
Day 7: Have a mock dialogue asking and answering 10 questions

Next steps

Once you master questions and negatives:

  1. Study Articles (a, an, the)
  2. Practice with Speaking: Building Confidence
  3. Review Grammar Drills

Related pages:

  • Core Grammar — full chapter
  • Be & Have
  • Present Simple
  • Articles
  • Dari companion

Articles (a, an, the)

Learning goal: Use a, an, and the correctly in sentences

Why articles matter

Articles are small words that come before nouns. They seem simple, but they change the meaning:

  • "I need a phone." (any phone)
  • "I need the phone." (a specific phone we both know about)

English uses articles constantly. Dari doesn't have articles like this, so they need focused practice.

Key fact: This is one of the hardest parts of English for Dari speakers. Be patient with yourself — it takes time.

The three articles

ArticleWhen to useExample
aBefore singular noun starting with consonant sounda book, a phone
anBefore singular noun starting with vowel soundan apple, an hour
theSpecific noun (both speaker and listener know which one)the book (we both know which book)

Indefinite articles: a / an

When to use "a" or "an"

Use a / an when:

  1. You mention something for the first time
  2. You mean any one of that thing (not a specific one)
  3. The noun is singular and countable

Choose "a" or "an" based on SOUND, not spelling:

ArticleUsed beforeExamples
aConsonant sounda book, a phone, a university (sounds like YOO)
anVowel sound (a, e, i, o, u sound)an apple, an hour (silent h), an orange

Examples with "a" / "an"

First mention:

  • I need a pen. (any pen)
  • She has a phone. (one phone, not specific)
  • I want an apple. (any apple)

One of many:

  • A student asked a question. (one student out of many)
  • I have a sister. (I might have more than one)
  • She is a teacher. (her profession)

Jobs and professions:

  • I am a student.
  • She is a doctor.
  • He is an engineer.

Dari tip: In Dari, you say "من معلم هستم" (I teacher am) with no article. In English, you MUST say "I am a teacher."

Definite article: the

When to use "the"

Use the when:

  1. Both you and the listener know which specific thing you're talking about
  2. There is only one of something
  3. You mentioned it before (second mention)

"The" works with:

  • Singular nouns: the book
  • Plural nouns: the books
  • Uncountable nouns: the water

Examples with "the"

Second mention (already introduced):

  • I bought a book. The book is very good.
  • She has a phone. The phone is old.

Only one exists (in context):

  • The sun is bright. (only one sun)
  • Please close the door. (the door of this room)
  • The internet is slow today. (the one we both use)

Specific, known to both people:

  • Can you give me the notebook? (we both know which notebook)
  • The teacher is kind. (we both know who the teacher is)

Superlatives and unique things:

  • She is the best student. (superlative)
  • The first word is hard. (order)

When NOT to use articles (zero article)

Don't use a/an/the in these cases:

1. Plural or uncountable nouns (general meaning)

  • Books are useful. (books in general, not specific books)
  • Water is important. (water in general)
  • Students study English. (students in general)

But: Use "the" if you mean specific ones:

  • The books on the table are mine. (specific books)
  • The water in this cup is cold. (specific water)

2. Names of people and places (usually)

  • Sara is my friend. (not a Sara or the Sara)
  • I live in Kabul. (not the Kabul)
  • I study English. (not an English or the English)

Exception: Some country names use "the":

  • the United States, the United Kingdom, the Netherlands

3. Abstract nouns (general concepts)

  • Love is powerful. (love in general)
  • Freedom is important.
  • Education changes lives.

But: Use "the" if you mean a specific instance:

  • The love she shows her family is beautiful. (specific love)

4. Meals (general)

  • I eat breakfast at 7 AM.
  • She has lunch at noon.

But: Use "the" if specific:

  • The breakfast we had yesterday was delicious.

Common patterns

Jobs and professions

  • I am a student.
  • She is an engineer.
  • He is a teacher.

Time expressions

  • once a week
  • twice a day
  • $10 an hour

Unique things

  • the sun, the moon, the earth
  • the sky, the sea, the ground

Directions and places in town

  • at the door, on the floor, in the corner
  • at the market, at the hospital (specific one)

But:

  • at home, at school, at work (no article)

Hazaragi tip: Even if articles don't exist in Hazaragi, think of "the" as meaning "that specific one" — it will help you remember when to use it.

Practice activities

Activity 1: Choose a or an

  1. ___ book (a)
  2. ___ apple (an)
  3. ___ hour (an)
  4. ___ university (a — sounds like YOO)
  5. ___ orange (an)

Activity 2: Fill in a, an, or the

  1. I have ___ phone. ___ phone is old. (a / The)
  2. She is ___ teacher. (a)
  3. ___ sun is bright. (The)
  4. I need ___ apple and ___ banana. (an / a)
  5. Can you close ___ door? (the)

Activity 3: Add articles where needed (or write "no article")

  1. ___ Students study English. (no article — general)
  2. I live in ___ Kabul. (no article — city name)
  3. I need ___ help. (no article — abstract/uncountable, unless you mean "the help you promised")
  4. She has ___ sister. (a)
  5. ___ Water is important. (no article — general)

Self-check quiz

  1. Complete: "I am ___ student." (a)
  2. Complete: "She has ___ old phone. ___ phone doesn't work." (an / The)
  3. When do you use "an" instead of "a"? (Before vowel sound)
  4. Fix error: "I like the books." (when meaning books in general) (I like books.)
  5. Do you say "the Kabul" or "Kabul"? (Kabul — no article for city names)

Common mistakes (Dari speakers)

MistakeWhy wrongCorrect
I am student.Missing article for professionI am a student.
I need the help."Help" is general hereI need help.
The books are good.Meaning books in generalBooks are good.
I have phone.Missing article for countable singularI have a phone.
I live in the Kabul.City names don't use "the"I live in Kabul.
She is teacher.Missing articleShe is a teacher.

Summary: Quick decision tree

Ask yourself:

  1. Is it countable and singular?

    • Yes → Use a/an or the
    • No (plural or uncountable) → Usually no article (unless specific)

  2. Is it the first time mentioning it?

    • Yes → Use a/an
    • No (we talked about it before) → Use the

  3. Do both people know which one?

    • Yes → Use the
    • No → Use a/an

  4. Is it a general statement?

    • Yes → No article (Books are good.)
    • No → Use the (The books on the table are good.)

Daily practice plan

Day 1: Write 10 sentences with "a"
Day 2: Write 10 sentences with "an"
Day 3: Write 10 sentences with "the" (second mention)
Day 4: Write 10 sentences with no article (general plurals)
Day 5: Mix all — write 15 sentences using a/an/the/no article
Day 6: Take a paragraph and underline every article; explain why it's there
Day 7: Write 1 paragraph about your day (10 sentences) — check articles carefully

Don't worry about perfection

Important message: Articles are difficult. Even advanced learners make mistakes. Native speakers will understand you even if you miss an article.

Priority:

  1. First, focus on a/an for jobs: "I am a student."
  2. Then, focus on the for second mentions.
  3. Over time, you'll develop a feeling for when to use them.

Next steps

Once you practice articles:

  1. Review all grammar topics in Core Grammar
  2. Move to Vocabulary Building
  3. Practice with Grammar Drills

Related pages:

  • Core Grammar — full chapter
  • Be & Have
  • Present Simple
  • Questions & Negatives
  • Dari companion

Vocabulary Building

Stage 1 → Stage 2 · Ongoing daily habit

Learning goals

  1. Learn high-frequency words in themed groups
  2. Use word families (act → action → active)
  3. Apply spaced repetition without paid apps
  4. Reach 300 words (month 2) and 800+ (month 6)
  5. Connect new words to speaking and writing same day

Why this skill matters

Grammar without words is an empty house. Vocabulary lets you say what you feel and need.

Ten new words today, used in one sentence each, beat fifty words memorized silently.

Level focus

Stage 1–3 (never stop learning words).

Step-by-step lessons

Lesson 1 — How to learn a new word (15 min)

For each word, save 4 parts:

PartExample: patient
Englishpatient
Meaning (simple)able to wait calmly
Example sentenceI am patient with my English.
Pronunciation notePAY-shunt (stress first syllable)

Same day: speak the sentence aloud.

Lesson 2 — Top 50 verbs (20 min)

be, have, do, say, get, make, go, know, take, see, come, think, look, want, give, use, find, tell, ask, work, seem, feel, try, leave, call, need, become, keep, let, begin, help, show, hear, play, run, move, live, believe, bring, happen, write, sit, stand, lose, pay, meet, include, continue, set, learn, change

Lesson 3 — Top 50 nouns for your life (20 min)

time, year, people, way, day, woman, man, child, world, life, hand, part, place, work, week, case, point, government, company, number, night, home, water, room, mother, father, area, money, story, fact, month, lot, right, study, book, eye, job, word, business, issue, side, kind, head, house, service, friend, father, power, hour, game, line, end, member, health, idea, food, student, teacher, language, phone, internet, message, problem, answer, question

Lesson 4 — Thematic sets (learn 10/week)

Home & daily life: kitchen, wash, clean, cook, sleep, wake, tired, busy, quiet, safe

Study: course, lesson, practice, test, mistake, improve, goal, plan, notebook, review

Health: doctor, medicine, pain, better, rest, exercise, body, heart, sick, well

Technology: download, app, Wi-Fi, password, screen, charge, battery, click, search, online

Emotions & strength: hope, courage, proud, worry, calm, strong, free, dream, choose, try again

Lesson 5 — Word families (15 min)

RootFamily
studystudent, studied, studying
helphelpful, helper, helpless (careful: negative meaning)
createcreative, creation, creator
educateeducation, educational, educator

Rule: Learn the verb first, then nouns/adjectives.

Lesson 6 — Spaced repetition (phone method) (15 min)

Day 1: Learn 10 words
Day 2: Review Day 1 + learn 10 new
Day 4: Review all
Day 7: Review all
Day 14: Review all

Tool: Notes app — title English words Week 3. Move mastered words to DONE section.

Lesson 7 — 1000-word path (overview)

MonthTarget active words
1150
2300

6800
121200+

“Active” = you can use it in a sentence, not only recognize it.

Examples

Word map for learn:

  • I learn English every day. (verb)
  • Learning takes time. (noun form)
  • She is a fast learner. (person)

Thematic paragraph:

I use my phone for online study. I download a lesson and practice in a quiet room. I feel proud when I improve.

Practice tasks

  1. Add 10 home words to your notebook with sentences.
  2. Review last week’s words before learning new ones.
  3. Describe your day using 15 verbs from Lesson 2.
  4. Drills: exercises/04-vocabulary-drills.md

Self-check questions

  1. What four parts should every new word entry have?
  2. What is spaced repetition?
  3. Give one word family from create.
  4. How many new words per day is realistic on a busy week?
  5. Why use new words in sentences the same day?

High-Frequency Words (1-300)

Learning goal: Master the 300 most common English words

Why these 300 words matter

These 300 words make up about 65% of all written English. Learn them well, and you can:

  • Understand basic texts
  • Have simple conversations
  • Express most daily needs
  • Build a foundation for grammar

Study strategy: Learn 10-15 words per day with example sentences. Review daily.

How to use this list

For each word:

  1. Read the English word
  2. Check the Dari meaning
  3. Study the example sentence
  4. Write your own sentence
  5. Say the sentence aloud

Don't just memorize translations. Use each word in a sentence the same day you learn it.

Full word list

See the complete list with Dari translations and examples in: Appendix: First 300 High-Frequency Words

That appendix includes:

  • All 300 words with Dari translations (دری)
  • Example sentence for each word
  • Words grouped by type (verb, noun, adjective, etc.)

Learning groups (suggested 20-day plan)

Days 1-2: Group 1 — Core pronouns & be (words 1-30)

I, you, he, she, it, we, they, be, have, do, say, get, make, go, know, take, see, come, think, look, want, give, use, find, tell, ask, work, need, feel, try

Practice: Write 5 sentences about yourself using these words.

Days 3-4: Group 2 — Essential verbs (words 31-60)

call, keep, let, begin, help, show, hear, play, run, move, live, believe, bring, happen, write, sit, stand, lose, pay, meet, include, continue, set, learn, change, lead, understand, watch, follow, stop

Practice: Describe your morning routine using 8 verbs.

Days 5-6: Group 3 — Common nouns — time & life (words 61-90)

time, year, people, way, day, man, world, life, hand, part, child, place, woman, work, week, case, point, number, group, problem, fact, month, lot, right, study, book, eye, job, word, business

Practice: Write about your day using 10 time/life words.

Days 7-8: Group 4 — More nouns — things & places (words 91-120)

issue, side, kind, head, house, service, friend, power, hour, game, line, end, member, law, car, city, name, president, minute, idea, body, information, water, room, mother, area, money, story, family, student

Practice: Describe your home and family using 10 words.

Days 9-10: Group 5 — Adjectives & states (words 121-150)

good, new, first, last, long, great, little, own, other, old, right, big, high, different, small, large, next, early, young, important, few, public, bad, same, able

Practice: Describe 5 things/people with adjectives.

Days 11-12: Group 6 — Connection words & more (words 151-180)

and, or, but, not, all, some, if, when, so, up, out, about, over, after, more, most, only, very, well, back, much, good, very, really, just, still, also, then

Practice: Write 8 sentences using "and," "but," "so," "when."

Days 13-14: Group 7 — Actions & states continued (words 181-210)

go, come, take, get, make, see, know, think, say, find, give, tell, ask, work, feel, become, leave, put, mean, keep, let, begin, seem, help, talk, turn, start, might, show, hear

Practice: Write 10 action sentences about your week.

Days 15-16: Group 8 — Questions & directions (words 211-240)

what, which, who, where, when, why, how, there, here, this, that, these, those, one, two, three, all, both, each, every, many, some, few, any, no, other, another

Practice: Ask 10 questions using question words.

Days 17-18: Group 9 — Prepositions & location (words 241-270)

in, on, at, to, for, of, with, from, by, about, as, into, through, during, before, after, above, below, under, over, between, among, around, near, far, up, down

Practice: Describe where 5 things are (in, on, under, near...).

Days 19-20: Group 10 — Final essentials (words 271-300)

can, will, would, could, should, may, might, must, shall, yes, no, ok, please, thank, sorry, hello, goodbye, help, stop, wait, listen, speak, read, write, open, close, begin, end, start, finish

Practice: Write polite requests using "please," "can," "could."

Study methods

Method 1: Flashcards (phone notes)

  • Line 1: English word
  • Line 2: Dari meaning + example
  • Review 20 words before bed

Method 2: Spaced repetition

  • Day 1: Learn 15 words
  • Day 2: Review Day 1 + learn 15 new
  • Day 4: Review all from Days 1-2
  • Day 7: Review all
  • Day 14: Final review

Method 3: Thematic sentences

Write one paragraph (8-10 sentences) using as many words from each group as possible.

Example (Group 1 words):

I am a student. I study English every day. I want to learn. I think it is important. I see my future. I have hope.

Method 4: Daily practice

  • Morning: Review yesterday's 15 words
  • Midday: Learn 15 new words
  • Evening: Use 5 new words in spoken sentences

Self-check quiz

After learning each group of 30 words:

  1. Can you write 10 of them from memory?
  2. Can you use 5 of them in sentences?
  3. Can you recognize them when you read them?

If yes to all three → move to next group.
If no → review for 2 more days before moving on.

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
Only memorizing translationsWrite example sentences in English
Learning 50 words in one dayStick to 10-15 per day for retention
Not reviewingReview yesterday's words before learning new ones
Silent study onlySay words aloud, use them in speech

Integration with grammar

As you learn words 1-300, practice them with grammar patterns:

  • Words 1-50: Practice with "be" (am/is/are)
  • Words 51-100: Practice with "have" and present simple
  • Words 101-150: Practice in questions
  • Words 151-200: Practice with negatives
  • Words 201-250: Practice with prepositions
  • Words 251-300: Practice with modal verbs (can, will, would)

Tracking progress

Create a simple tracker in your notes:

Week 1: ✅ Words 1-75 (Groups 1-2.5)
Week 2: ✅ Words 76-150 (Groups 2.5-5)
Week 3: 🔲 Words 151-225 (Groups 6-7.5)
Week 4: 🔲 Words 226-300 (Groups 7.5-10)

Next steps

Once you master words 1-300:

  1. Move to High-Frequency Words (301-700)
  2. Practice with Memory Techniques
  3. Use words in Speaking and Writing

  • Vocabulary Building — full chapter
  • High-Frequency Words (301-700)

High-Frequency Words (301-700)

Learning goal: Expand vocabulary to 700 most common words

Why words 301-700 matter

Once you know the first 300 words, these next 400 words:

  • Increase your comprehension to 75-80% of written English
  • Let you express more complex ideas
  • Help you understand longer conversations
  • Enable you to read simple articles and stories

Study pace: Continue with 10-15 words per day. At this rate, you'll finish in about 4 weeks.

How this level is different

Words 1-300 are mostly:

  • Simple, everyday words
  • Single-syllable or two-syllable words
  • Basic nouns, verbs, adjectives

Words 301-700 include:

  • More specific nouns (government, education, health)
  • Action verbs (decide, explain, develop)
  • Abstract concepts (idea, reason, result)
  • More adjectives and adverbs

You'll need to practice these words in sentences more actively.

Full word list

The complete list with Dari translations and examples is in: Appendix: Words 301-1000

That appendix includes:

  • All 400 words (301-700) with Dari translations
  • Example sentence for each word
  • Thematic groupings

Suggested learning groups (4-week plan)

Week 1: Words 301-400 (civic & social words)

Focus: government, society, community, relationships

Sample words: government, president, law, rule, country, state, city, town, community, society, public, private, group, team, member, leader, follow, join, support, vote, agree, decide, choose, change, improve, develop, grow, increase, build, create, organize

Practice: Write about your community or country using 15 words from this group.

Week 2: Words 401-500 (education & work)

Focus: learning, jobs, actions, progress

Sample words: education, school, university, course, lesson, teach, learn, study, practice, test, exam, pass, fail, grade, knowledge, skill, ability, improve, progress, develop, job, work, career, business, office, manager, worker, employee, company, service, product, sell, buy, pay, cost, price, money, dollar, save, spend, earn

Practice: Describe your study routine or a job using 15 words.

Week 3: Words 501-600 (description & comparison)

Focus: adjectives, adverbs, comparisons

Sample words: different, similar, same, equal, various, several, many, much, few, little, more, most, less, least, better, best, worse, worst, bigger, smaller, older, newer, faster, slower, stronger, weaker, higher, lower, longer, shorter, earlier, later, easier, harder, simpler, clearer, important, necessary, possible, impossible, difficult, easy, hard, soft, heavy, light, full, empty, clean, dirty

Practice: Compare two things (phone vs computer, morning vs night, etc.)

Week 4: Words 601-700 (time, reason, result)

Focus: connecting ideas, explaining cause and effect

Sample words: because, so, therefore, since, although, however, yet, still, already, just, only, even, almost, quite, rather, perhaps, maybe, probably, certainly, surely, truly, really, actually, finally, eventually, suddenly, recently, ago, before, after, during, while, until, since, for, reason, cause, result, effect, happen, occur, lead, follow, continue, stop, begin, end, finish, complete

Practice: Write 10 sentences explaining why you do things ("I study because..." / "I practice so that...")

Study methods for this level

Method 1: Sentence mining

  • Read a simple English article
  • Find 5 words from the 301-700 list
  • Write your own sentences with those words

Method 2: Thematic paragraphs

Write one paragraph per theme:

  • Education: Use 10 school/study words
  • Family & society: Use 10 relationship words
  • Goals: Use 10 future/action words

Method 3: Synonym practice

Many words 301-700 are synonyms of words 1-300:

  • big → large, huge, enormous
  • small → little, tiny
  • good → nice, fine, excellent
  • bad → poor, terrible, awful

Learn word families together.

Method 4: Collocations (words that go together)

  • make a decision
  • take a test
  • do homework
  • give advice
  • pay attention
  • break a rule

Learn verbs with their common noun partners.

Integration with grammar

As you learn words 301-700, practice advanced patterns:

  • Past tense: "Yesterday, I decided to study more."
  • Future: "I will explain it tomorrow."
  • Modals: "You should improve your skills."
  • Comparatives: "This method is more effective."
  • Conditionals: "If you practice, you will improve."

Self-check quiz (after each 100 words)

  1. Can you write 20 words from this group from memory?
  2. Can you use 10 of them in sentences without looking?
  3. Can you explain 5 words in simple English (without Dari)?
  4. Can you recognize them when reading?

If yes to all → move to next group.
If no → review for 3 more days.

Common challenges

Challenge 1: Similar words

Many words sound similar or have close meanings:

  • affect / effect
  • accept / except
  • advice / advise

Solution: Learn one at a time with clear examples.

Challenge 2: Abstract words

Words like "concept," "principle," "theory" are harder because they're abstract.

Solution: Connect them to concrete examples from your life.

Challenge 3: Longer words

Words like "government" (3 syllables) or "education" (4 syllables) are harder to remember.

Solution: Break them into syllables: gov-ern-ment, ed-u-ca-tion. Practice stress.

Daily practice routine (15 minutes)

Morning (5 min): Review yesterday's 10-15 words
Midday (5 min): Learn 10-15 new words
Evening (5 min): Write 3 sentences using new words + say them aloud

Tracking progress

Week 1: ✅ Words 301-400 (civic & social)
Week 2: ✅ Words 401-500 (education & work)
Week 3: 🔲 Words 501-600 (description & comparison)
Week 4: 🔲 Words 601-700 (time, reason, result)

Milestone: After mastering 700 words, you can understand about 75-80% of everyday English text.

Vocabulary notebook tips

For each word at this level, write:

Word: government
Dari: حکومت
Stress: GOV-ern-ment (stress first syllable)
Example: The government helps people.
My sentence: I think a good government is important.
Word family: govern (verb), governor (noun)

Next steps

Once you master words 301-700:

  1. Move to Words 701-1000 (Self-Fill)
  2. Practice with Memory Techniques
  3. Use words in Reading Strategies to learn from context
  4. Apply in Writing: Paragraphs

Related pages:

  • Vocabulary Building — full chapter
  • High-Frequency Words (1-300)
  • Words 701-1000 (Self-Fill)
  • Memory Techniques
  • Full word list with Dari
  • Dari companion

Words 701-1000 (Self-Fill Template)

Learning goal: Reach 1000+ active vocabulary words

Why words 701-1000 matter

Reaching 1000 words is a major milestone:

  • You can understand about 80-85% of everyday English
  • You can hold conversations on most common topics
  • You can read intermediate-level texts
  • You're ready for more advanced grammar and writing

These final 300 words should be chosen based on your interests and goals:

  • Technology & internet
  • Health & medical
  • Education & career
  • Hobbies & interests
  • Current events

Important: At this level, you choose which words to learn. Focus on words you actually need and will use.

How this level works

Words 1-300: Essential foundation — everyone needs these
Words 301-700: High-frequency — most learners need these
Words 701-1000: Personalized — you choose based on your goals

Finding your words 701-1000

Strategy 1: Learn from what you read

When you read English articles, stories, or lessons:

  1. Highlight unknown words
  2. Look up the meaning
  3. If the word appears 3+ times in different texts → add it to your list
  4. Write it in your vocabulary notebook

This is the most natural way to build vocabulary at this level.

Strategy 2: Thematic word sets

Choose 3-5 themes that matter to you, then learn 50-100 words per theme.

Example themes:

Technology & Internet (50-100 words)

  • hardware, software, application, program, download, upload, install, delete, connect, network, Wi-Fi, password, username, account, profile, settings, device, screen, keyboard, mouse, cursor, file, folder, document, save, print, scan, email, attach, send, receive, browser, search, link, website, page, blog, social media, post, share, comment, like, follow, login, logout, update, backup, virus, security, privacy, data, cloud, online, offline

Health & Wellness (50-100 words)

  • health, healthy, unhealthy, medical, medicine, doctor, nurse, hospital, clinic, patient, disease, illness, sick, pain, hurt, injury, wound, treat, treatment, cure, heal, recover, recovery, symptom, fever, cough, cold, flu, headache, stomach, chest, heart, lung, blood, bone, muscle, skin, exercise, fitness, diet, nutrition, vitamin, protein, vegetable, fruit, meal, breakfast, lunch, dinner, sleep, rest, tired, energy, stress, relax, mental, physical, body, mind

Education & Career (50-100 words)

  • education, educational, learn, learner, teach, teacher, student, study, school, university, college, course, class, lesson, subject, topic, exam, test, quiz, grade, pass, fail, degree, diploma, certificate, knowledge, skill, ability, talent, career, job, work, profession, professional, employer, employee, manager, supervisor, colleague, coworker, office, company, business, organization, project, task, deadline, goal, achieve, success, fail, experience, qualification, apply, application, interview, hire, resign, retire, salary, income, promote, promotion

Daily Life & Home (50-100 words)

  • house, home, apartment, building, floor, ceiling, wall, window, door, room, bedroom, bathroom, kitchen, living room, furniture, table, chair, bed, sofa, closet, shelf, clean, dirty, wash, sweep, dust, organize, repair, fix, broken, rent, own, landlord, tenant, neighbor, neighborhood, community, street, road, address, store, shop, market, buy, sell, price, cost, expensive, cheap, pay, cash, card, receipt, grocery, vegetables, fruit, meat, chicken, rice, oil, spice, cook, meal, dish, taste, delicious

Technology & Communication (50-100 words)

  • communicate, communication, message, text, call, phone, smartphone, email, letter, mail, post, send, receive, reply, answer, respond, information, news, media, newspaper, magazine, article, report, story, radio, television, TV, channel, program, show, video, film, movie, watch, listen, read, write, speak, talk, conversation, discuss, discussion, debate, argue, argument, opinion, idea, thought, believe, agree, disagree, true, false, fact, lie, truth, honest, dishonest

Strategy 3: Academic word list

If you plan to:

  • Take online courses in English
  • Apply for scholarships
  • Read academic materials

Learn from the Academic Word List (AWL) — 570 important words used in higher education:

  • analyze, approach, area, assess, assume, authority, available, benefit, concept, consist, context, contract, create, data, define, derive, distribute, economy, environment, establish, estimate, evidence, factor, finance, formula, function, identify, income, indicate, individual, interpret, involve, issue, labor, legal, legislate, major, method, occur, percent, period, policy, principle, procedure, process, require, research, respond, role, section, sector, significant, similar, source, specific, structure, theory, vary

These words are essential for academic success.

Strategy 4: Professional vocabulary

Choose words for your field or career goal:

  • Remote work: freelance, client, project, deadline, invoice, payment, platform, portfolio, proposal, contract
  • Teaching: curriculum, assessment, feedback, evaluation, pedagogy, methodology
  • Healthcare: diagnosis, prescription, treatment, prevention, immunization
  • Business: strategy, marketing, customer, product, service, profit, revenue, expense

Your personal vocabulary log (template)

Create a document or note called "My 701-1000 Words" with this format:

### Word 701: [English word]
- Dari: [meaning]
- Category: [technology / health / education / etc.]
- Example: [sentence from where you found it]
- My sentence: [your own sentence]
- Date learned: [date]

### Word 702: [English word]
...

Goal: Add 2-5 words per day from your reading and interests.

How to learn words at this level

1. Learn in context (always)

Don't just memorize word lists. Learn words from:

  • Articles you read
  • Videos you watch (with subtitles)
  • Conversations you hear

2. Focus on collocations

At this level, learn how words work together:

  • strong coffee (not "powerful coffee")
  • make a decision (not "do a decision")
  • take a test (not "make a test")
  • do homework (not "make homework")

3. Learn word families

When you learn a new word, learn its family:

  • educate (verb) → education (noun) → educational (adjective) → educator (noun)
  • communicate (verb) → communication (noun) → communicative (adjective)

4. Use new words within 24 hours

  • Write a sentence
  • Say it aloud
  • Use it in a message or conversation (if safe)

Self-assessment

After learning 100 words at this level:

Can you:

  1. Write 30 words from memory?
  2. Use 20 words in sentences without looking?
  3. Explain 10 words in simple English?
  4. Recognize them when reading?
  5. Use them in speaking or writing naturally?

If yes → you're progressing well.
If no → slow down, focus on fewer words, use them more.

Resources for finding words 701-1000

Simple English websites

  • Simple English Wikipedia (simple.wikipedia.org)
  • VOA Learning English (learningenglish.voanews.com)
  • BBC Learning English (bbclearningenglish.com)

Word frequency tools

  • Use online frequency lists (search "1000 most common English words")
  • Focus on words ranked 701-1000

From your interests

  • If you like technology → read simple tech articles, note new words
  • If you like health → read health tips in English
  • If you like stories → read graded readers (A2-B1 level)

Common mistakes at this level

MistakeFix
Learning words you'll never useChoose words from your actual reading and interests
Only passive recognitionForce yourself to use new words in writing/speaking
Ignoring collocationsLearn phrases, not just single words
Translating too literallySome words don't translate directly — learn meaning from context

Milestone: 1000 words

Congratulations! When you reach 1000 active words:

  • You're at solid A2/B1 level vocabulary
  • You can handle most daily conversations
  • You can read intermediate texts with a dictionary
  • You're ready for more complex grammar and writing

What "active" means:

  • You can use the word in a sentence
  • You can spell it
  • You know common collocations
  • You recognize it when reading or listening

Don't aim for perfect pronunciation or 100% accuracy — aim for confident use.

Next steps

After reaching 1000 words:

  1. Stop counting — vocabulary learning becomes natural from reading and listening
  2. Learn words in context from materials at your level
  3. Focus on using vocabulary in Writing and Speaking
  4. Review Memory Techniques for long-term retention
  5. Move to Intermediate Growth for advanced grammar

Maintenance: Don't forget words 1-700

Weekly review:

  • Monday: Review 50 words from 1-300
  • Wednesday: Review 50 words from 301-700
  • Friday: Review 50 words from 701-1000

Monthly challenge: Write a 200-word essay using words from all three levels.

Memory Techniques for Vocabulary

Learning goal: Remember new words faster and longer

Why memory techniques matter

Learning 1000+ words requires smart repetition, not just hard work. Memory techniques help you:

  • Remember words after fewer repetitions
  • Keep words in long-term memory
  • Connect new words to what you already know
  • Make learning more enjoyable

Key principle: Your brain remembers stories, patterns, and emotions better than random lists.

Technique 1: Spaced repetition

What it is: Review words at increasing intervals.

Why it works: Your brain strengthens memories each time you recall them just before forgetting.

How to do it:

DayAction
Day 1Learn 10 new words
Day 2Review Day 1 + learn 10 new
Day 4Review Day 1 & 2
Day 7Review all from Days 1-4
Day 14Review all
Day 30Final review

Phone method (no app needed):

  1. Create a note called "English Words — Week 1"
  2. Write each word with example
  3. Set reminders: Day 2, Day 4, Day 7, Day 14
  4. When reminder pops up, quiz yourself before looking

Technique 2: The keyword method (for Dari speakers)

What it is: Connect English word to a similar-sounding Dari word, then create a mental image.

Example:

English word: "bread"
Sounds like Dari: "برید" (berid — doesn't mean bread, but sounds similar)
Mental image: Picture cutting (برید) bread
Result: When you hear "bread," you think of cutting → bread

More examples:

EnglishSounds like (Dari/Hazaragi)Image
nameنامDirect match!
motherمادر (mādar)Direct match!
sisterسیستر (sounds similar)Your sister
timeتایم (borrowed word)Clock time

For words that don't sound similar: Create an image with meaning.

English: "hope"
Dari meaning: امید (omid)
Image: Picture yourself with a bright light (symbol of hope/omid)

Dari tip: Many English words are borrowed into Dari (computer, internet, telephone, etc.). You already know these — just practice English pronunciation!

Technique 3: Word families (learn groups, not singles)

What it is: Learn related words together.

Example family: "study"

WordTypeExample
studyverbI study English.
studentnoun (person)I am a student.
studiousadjectiveShe is a studious person.
studiesnoun (subjects)I love language studies.

When you learn one word, learn its whole family — your brain stores them as a connected network.

More families:

  • teach → teacher → taught → teaching
  • learn → learner → learned → learning → lesson
  • work → worker → worked → working
  • write → writer → wrote → written → writing

Technique 4: Mnemonics (memory tricks)

What it is: Create a story or phrase to remember spelling or meaning.

Example: Remember "necessary"

  • "One collar, two sleeves" → necessary

Example: Remember "because"

  • Big Elephants Can Always Understand Small Elephants → BECAUSE

Create your own:

  • Make them funny or personal
  • The weirder the story, the better you'll remember

Technique 5: Visualization (mental images)

What it is: Create a vivid mental picture for each word.

Example: "hope"

  • Close your eyes
  • Picture a sunrise after a long night
  • Feel the warmth
  • Connect: hope = sunrise = new beginning

Example: "courage"

  • Picture yourself standing strong
  • Feel your heart beating
  • See yourself taking a difficult step forward

For abstract words (like freedom, love, hope), connect them to:

  • A personal memory
  • A symbol (dove = peace, heart = love, sunrise = hope)
  • A person who represents it

Technique 6: Personalization (make it about YOUR life)

What it is: Use new words to describe your own life.

Don't: "The student studies English."
Do: "I study English every morning in my quiet room."

Example set:

WordGeneric sentence (forget easily)Personal sentence (remember better)
tiredShe is tired.I am tired after studying for two hours.
happyHe is happy.I feel happy when I learn a new word.
hopeHope is important.I have hope for my future education.

Your brain remembers personal experiences better than abstract facts.

Technique 7: Collocations (words that go together)

What it is: Learn words in their natural partnerships.

Don't learn: make, do, take, give (separately)
Do learn:

  • make a mistake, a decision, a plan, progress, a difference
  • do homework, exercises, your best, the dishes, research
  • take a test, a break, time, a photo, notes
  • give advice, an answer, a speech, permission, support

When you learn a verb, write 3-5 nouns it commonly pairs with.

Technique 8: The memory palace (for advanced learners)

What it is: Associate words with locations in a familiar place (your house).

How:

  1. Choose a familiar route in your home (door → hallway → kitchen → bedroom)
  2. Place vocabulary words at each location with a vivid image
  3. When you need to recall, mentally walk through your house

Example:

  • Door: "open" — picture the door opening with golden light
  • Hallway: "walk" — see yourself walking slowly
  • Kitchen: "cook" — smell the bread cooking
  • Bedroom: "sleep" — see yourself resting peacefully

This technique is powerful but takes practice. Try it once you've mastered simpler methods.

Technique 9: Active recall (test yourself)

What it is: Quiz yourself BEFORE looking at answers.

Don't: Read word list 10 times
Do: Read once, then cover the answers and try to recall

Method:

  1. Write English word on one line
  2. Write Dari + example on next line
  3. Cover the answer
  4. Try to recall meaning
  5. Check
  6. If wrong → mark for review tomorrow

Research shows: Testing yourself is 3x more effective than passive review.

Technique 10: Emotional connection

What it is: Connect words to emotions or important moments.

Example: You learn the word "courage" (جرات) on a day when you do something brave (like starting English lessons despite fear).

Every time you see "courage," you'll remember: "This is the word I learned the day I started my journey."

Strong emotions = strong memories.

Daily vocabulary routine (10 minutes)

Morning (3 min): Review yesterday's words (active recall — cover answers)
Midday (4 min): Learn 10 new words with personal sentences
Evening (3 min): Quiz yourself on all new words from today

Common memory mistakes

MistakeWhy it failsBetter approach
Reading lists 10 timesPassive, no mental effortTest yourself (active recall)
Learning 50 words in one dayOverwhelms memory10-15 words per day, spaced out
No reviewMemory fadesReview Day 2, 4, 7, 14, 30
Only translatingNo context or connectionUse in personal sentences
Silent study onlyNo multi-sensory inputSay aloud, write, visualize

Memory helpers

Use all senses:

  • See the word written
  • Hear the word spoken (record yourself)
  • Say the word aloud
  • Write the word
  • Feel the meaning (visualize)

Create word webs:

         study
        /  |  \
    student  studies  studio
       |
    studious

Use colors:

  • Verbs in blue
  • Nouns in green
  • Adjectives in red

Visual organization helps memory.

Apps & tools (optional)

If you have reliable internet:

  • Anki (spaced repetition flashcards — free)
  • Quizlet (flashcards)
  • Memrise (memory techniques built-in)

But: You don't need apps. A simple notes app + calendar reminders works fine.

Tracking what works for YOU

After 2 weeks, check:

  • Which technique helps you remember best?
  • Which words stick easily? (Why?)
  • Which words keep slipping? (Try a different technique)

Everyone's brain is different. Find what works for you, then use that method consistently.

Long-term maintenance

Monthly review:

  • First Sunday of month: Review 100 random words
  • Use them in a 200-word essay

When you forget a word:

  • Don't feel bad
  • Learn it again (this time it sticks better)
  • Use it 5 times in the next 2 days

Forgetting is normal. Relearning is faster.

Next steps

Apply these memory techniques to:

  1. High-Frequency Words (1-300)
  2. High-Frequency Words (301-700)
  3. Words 701-1000
  4. Any new words you encounter in Reading or Listening

Related pages:

  • Vocabulary Building — full chapter
  • High-Frequency Words (1-300)
  • Study Strategies — general learning techniques
  • Dari companion

Pronunciation (American English)

Stage 1 → Stage 4 · Daily 5–10 min alongside other skills

Learning goals

  1. Hear and produce American vowels (ship vs sheep)
  2. Pronounce th, r, and w more clearly
  3. Use word stress so listeners understand you
  4. Fix Dari-influenced sound habits
  5. Use your phone to record and compare

Why this skill matters

Good grammar with unclear sounds still blocks communication. Clear pronunciation builds confidence — people listen to your ideas, not only your accent.

Goal: understood, not perfect. Native-like accent is optional.

Level focus

Stage 1 starts; refine through Stage 4.

Step-by-step lessons

Lesson 1 — American English basics (15 min)

  • R is stronger than in British English: water, far
  • T between vowels sounds like quick d in casual speech: water, better (later stage)
  • Final consonants: try to finish the sound: help, not hel

Lesson 2 — Vowel pairs (20 min)

SoundExampleCommon mistake
/ɪ/ short ishipsounds like sheep
/iː/ long eesheep
/ɛ/bed
/æ/cat
/ʌ/cup
/ɑː/father

Practice sentence:
I sit in a big city and drink tea.

Say slowly. Record. Listen.

Lesson 3 — Consonants for Dari speakers (20 min)

TH (voiceless) — thank, think, three

  • Tongue lightly between teeth, air out
  • Not tank or sank

TH (voiced) — this, that, mother

  • Tongue between teeth, voice on

P vs F — pat vs fat — lips vs teeth

V vs W — very vs west — teeth on lip for v

H — he, home — breathe out, not silent

Dari note: Sounds that do not exist in Dari need daily 2-minute drills. Short daily beats long weekly.

Lesson 4 — Word stress (15 min)

English stressed syllable is louder and longer.

WordStress (CAPS)
PHOtographpho-TO-graphy (shift!)
comPUter
eduCAtion
paTIENT

Rule: Stress wrong syllable → word sounds foreign or unclear.

Practice: Clap on stressed syllable: com-PU-ter

Lesson 5 — Sentence rhythm (15 min)

Content words (nouns, verbs) stronger; function words (a, the, to) softer.

  • I want to study English.
  • She lives at home.

Read aloud with exaggeration first, then natural speed.

Lesson 6 — Phone recording routine (10 min daily)

  1. Read 3 sentences from any module
  2. Record voice memo
  3. Listen — note one sound to fix tomorrow
  4. Optional: compare to slow American audio (see 15-resources.md)
  5. Privacy: store in locked/private folder; delete monthly

Examples

Minimal pairs (train your ear):

AB
shipsheep
live (verb)leave
veryberry (different vowels)

Introduction with clear stress:

HEL-lo. MY NAME IS Zahra. I STUD-y ENG-lish at HOME.

Then make it natural without losing key stresses.

Worksheet: assets/worksheets/worksheet-phonics-blends.md

Practice tasks

  1. 2-minute th drill: thank, this, that, mother, think.
  2. Record introduction — listen once — repeat.
  3. Clap stress on 10 words from 04-vocabulary-building.md.
  4. Drills: exercises/05-pronunciation-drills.md

Self-check questions

  1. What is the difference between ship and sheep?
  2. Where is your tongue for thank?
  3. Why does word stress matter?
  4. How can you practice safely on a shared phone?
  5. Is perfect accent required for good communication?

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
Replacing th with t or sSlow drill 20 reps
Dropping final consonantsExaggerate final sound 10 times
Flat stress on all syllablesClap + CAPS stress
Never listening to yourselfRecord 3x/week minimum

Milestone goals

Study habits

  • 5 min before main lesson — one sound only
  • Same sound for 3 days before switching
  • Praise one improvement per week (not one failure)
