Self-Recording Practice

Learning goal: Use your phone to track speaking progress

Why record yourself?

Your ears hear yourself differently when you speak vs when you listen to a recording. Recording lets you:

  • Hear your real pronunciation
  • Track improvement over time
  • Identify specific problems
  • Build confidence through visible progress

Fact: Most learners are surprised their English sounds better than they thought!

How to record safely (privacy first)

Storage tips

  • Create a private folder: "English Practice" or similar
  • Password-protect if your phone has folder locks
  • Use private/encrypted voice memo apps if available
  • Delete old recordings monthly (or keep best ones only)

Sharing

  • Do NOT post publicly unless you explicitly want to
  • If sharing with a study partner: use secure messaging (Signal, WhatsApp)
  • If using language exchange apps: check app privacy settings

What to record

Week 1: Introduction (30 seconds)

"Hello. My name is [name]. I am learning English. I study at home every day. I want to improve my speaking."

Week 2-4: Daily routine (1 minute)

Describe your morning, afternoon, or evening in simple sentences.

Month 2-3: Topics (2 minutes)

  • My family
  • My goals
  • Why I learn English
  • A memory
  • My favorite book/show

Month 4+: Advanced (3-5 minutes)

  • Explain a concept
  • Give your opinion with reasons
  • Tell a story
  • Describe a process

Recording routine (5-10 minutes)

Step 1: Choose a topic or sentence to practice
Step 2: Practice saying it 2-3 times (no recording yet)
Step 3: Record
Step 4: Listen once
Step 5: Note ONE thing done well + ONE thing to improve
Step 6: Re-record (optional) if you want
Step 7: Save or delete based on your privacy comfort

What to listen for

1. Clarity

  • Can you understand your own words?
  • Are final consonants clear? (stop, test, hand)

2. Rhythm and stress

  • Do content words stand out? (I STUDY ENGLISH DAILY)
  • Or do all words sound equally loud? (needs work)

3. Specific sounds

  • TH sounds (thank, this)
  • V vs W (very, water)
  • Vowel length (ship vs sheep)

4. Pace

  • Too fast? (hard to understand)
  • Too slow? (unnatural pauses)
  • Just right? (clear with natural pauses)

5. Confidence

  • Do you sound hesitant or confident?
  • Do you pause mid-sentence often?

Don't criticize harshly. Note areas to practice, then celebrate that you tried.

Tracking progress: Before & After

Month 1 recording

  • Save your first 30-second introduction
  • Label it "Month 1 — Intro"

Month 3 recording

  • Record the same introduction again
  • Compare with Month 1
  • Notice improvements (even small ones!)

This is powerfully motivating. You WILL sound better.

Common recording mistakes

MistakeFix
Only recording once, never listeningListen to at least 20% of your recordings
Being too harsh on yourselfNote ONE good thing before noting problems
Never re-recordingIt's okay to try 2-3 times
Recording material too hardStart simple, build complexity
Not dating recordingsLabel with date or month — track progress!

Weekly recording plan

DayTopicLength
MonIntroduction30 sec
WedDaily routine1 min
FriOpinion on one topic1-2 min

Total time per week: 10-15 minutes recording, 5 minutes listening.

Recording for specific goals

Goal: Improve pronunciation

  • Record 10 sentences with problem sounds (TH, V, R)
  • Listen: Did you produce those sounds?
  • Practice the ones you missed
  • Re-record next week

Goal: Build fluency

  • Record yourself speaking for 2 minutes without stopping (even if you make mistakes)
  • Goal = flow, not perfection
  • Track: Can you speak longer without pausing awkwardly?

Goal: Prepare for conversation

  • Record answers to common questions:
    • What is your name?
    • Where are you from?
    • What do you do?
    • Why are you learning English?
  • Practice until answers feel automatic

Apps for recording (optional)

Built-in voice memos (iPhone/Android) work fine. But if you want more features:

  • Voice Recorder apps with playback speed control
  • Note-taking apps with voice recording (Evernote, OneNote)
  • Language learning apps with recording feature (some have feedback)

Privacy reminder: Read app privacy policies before using.

Advanced: Transcribe your own recording

Challenge (Month 3+):

  1. Record yourself speaking for 1 minute
  2. Listen and write down EXACTLY what you said (dictation of yourself!)
  3. Check grammar, spelling, word choice
  4. Notice patterns: Do you always use simple words? Do you repeat "and" too much?

This reveals your speaking patterns.

Recording + other skills

Recording + Shadowing

  • Shadow a sentence from audio
  • Record yourself shadowing
  • Compare your version to the original
  • Goal: Match rhythm and stress

Recording + Pronunciation

  • After practicing a specific sound (TH, V, R), record yourself
  • Listen: Did you produce it correctly?

Recording + Writing

  • Write a short paragraph
  • Read it aloud and record
  • Check: Does it sound natural, or too written/formal?

Self-check: Am I using recording effectively?

After 4 weeks:

  1. Do I record at least once per week? ✅ / ❌
  2. Do I listen to my recordings? ✅ / ❌
  3. Do I note improvements? ✅ / ❌
  4. Do I store recordings safely? ✅ / ❌
  5. Do I feel more confident hearing my own voice? ✅ / ❌

If mostly ✅ → keep going, increase recording frequency
If mostly ❌ → start with once a week, listen to every recording

Encouragement

Hearing your own voice feels weird at first. Everyone experiences this, even native speakers.

Week 1: "Do I really sound like that?"
Week 2: "This is uncomfortable."
Week 3: "I'm getting used to it."
Week 4: "I can hear improvement!"

The discomfort fades. The progress stays.

Next steps

  1. Record your introduction TODAY (30 seconds)
  2. Review Building Confidence if recording feels scary
  3. Try Shadowing and record yourself shadowing
  4. Use Speaking Drills for topics

Related pages:

  • Speaking — full chapter
  • Building Confidence
  • Shadowing Technique
  • Pronunciation
  • Dari companion

Reading Skills

Stage 1 → Stage 4 · 10–15 min daily

Learning goals

  1. Use phonics (letters → sounds) at Stage 0–1
  2. Move from letters to graded texts
  3. Read messages and short articles with strategies
  4. Scan for information (time, name, price)
  5. Infer meaning from context
  6. Build reading speed without skipping comprehension

Why this skill matters

Reading feeds vocabulary and grammar at your speed. On a phone, you can read English anywhere — privacy, safety, independence.

Read a little every day. One paragraph beats zero pages.

Level focus

Stage 1: words/sentences · Stage 2: paragraphs · Stage 3+: articles, news

Step-by-step lessons

Lesson 0 — Phonics: letters to sounds (20 min)

For Stage 0–1. Reading English starts with linking letters to sounds.

Consonants (common sounds):

LetterSound inExample
Bbookbook
Ddayday
Ffoodfood
Hhomehome
Kkeykey
Llearnlearn
Mmothermother
Nnamename
Sstudystudy
Tteatea

Short vowels (practice slowly):

LetterSoundWord
A/æ/cat, hat
E/ɛ/bed, red
I/ɪ/sit, big
O/ɒ/ or /ɑ/hot, go (American varies)
U/ʌ/cup, sun

Blends (two letters, one flow):

  • sh — she, wish
  • ch — child, teach
  • th — thank, this (see 05-pronunciation.md)
  • st — study, stop
  • br — bread, brother

Phone practice (5 min daily):

  1. Pick 5 words from 02-beginner-foundations.md.
  2. Say each sound by sound, then whole word.
  3. Type the word — connect eyes → fingers → mouth.

Dari note: حروف لاتین صداهای متفاوت دارند — حرف a در cat و name یکسان نیست. Be patient; phonics takes 2–4 weeks.

Simple decode steps:

  1. Look at first letter → say sound
  2. Move left to right
  3. Blend: s + t + u + d → study

When a word is long, split: re-mem-ber (later stage).

Lesson 1 — Reading levels (10 min)

LevelText type
ASingle words, labels
B1–3 sentences
CShort dialogues
D100–200 word stories
ENews, blogs, chapters

Rule: Know ~80% of words or guessable from context.

Lesson 2 — Decode strategy (15 min)

For hard words:

  1. Skip — read the sentence anyway
  2. Guess from context
  3. Look up only key words (do not stop every line)
  4. Write one English definition in your notebook

Lesson 3 — Graded reading routine (20 min)

StepAction
1Skim title and first sentence
2Read full text once — no dictionary
3Circle unknown words (mental note)
4Read again — guess meanings
5Write 2 sentences: main idea + my opinion

Lesson 4 — Scanning (15 min)

Find fast:

  • phone number, email, date, price, name

Practice text:

Class starts on March 12 at 9:00 a.m. Email learn@example.com for help. Fee: free.

Questions: When? What time? Email? Cost?

Lesson 5 — Inference (15 min)

Text: “Sara’s hands shook as she opened the test results. Then she smiled.”

Infer: Sara was nervous; news was good.

Practice: Read short stories — ask How did she feel? Why?

Lesson 6 — Phone reading habits (10 min)

  • Increase font size for comfort
  • Night mode for eyes
  • Bookmark one article — finish tomorrow (not 10 half-read)
  • Download PDF/HTML for offline (15-resources.md)

Sample graded text (Stage 1–2)

My Study Corner

I study English in a small room at home. I use my phone and a notebook. I learn thirty minutes every day. Some days I am tired, but I continue. My sister sometimes listens and smiles. I want to read books in English one day. I know patience is important. Every word I learn is a step forward.

Comprehension (try before keys in exercises):

  1. Where does she study?
  2. How long each day?
  3. Who listens sometimes?
  4. What is her dream?

Practice tasks

  1. Read sample text twice; answer questions.
  2. Scan a real message (email/chat) for one fact.
  3. Write 3 sentences summarizing your day (reading + writing link).
  4. Drills: exercises/08-reading-drills.md

Self-check questions

  1. What is the 80% word rule?
  2. What are the 5 steps of graded reading?
  3. What is scanning vs deep reading?
  4. What is inference?
  5. Why not look up every unknown word?

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
Text too hardDrop one level
Translating every word in headSummarize in simple English
No re-readSecond read builds speed
Only social media slangAdd one graded paragraph/week

Milestone goals

WhenGoal
Month 110 short texts
Month 3150-word story understood
Month 6News article with notes
Month 12Book chapter or long blog

Study habits

  • Read aloud sometimes — links to speaking
  • Keep a reading log (title + date) in tracker
  • Favorite topic (health, study, stories) sustains habit

Extended practice

Phonics week: 10 min/day Letter-sound drills from Lesson 0.

Ten stories: appendices/graded-readings.md — one per week at your level (A–E).

Inference drill: After each story, answer: How did the person feel? Why?

Unit quiz: exercises/unit-quiz-08-reading.md

Reading Strategies

Learning goal: Read more effectively and efficiently

Core reading strategies

Strategy 1: Preview before reading

Look at title, headings, first sentences to predict content.

Strategy 2: Scan for specific information

Find names, dates, numbers quickly without reading everything.

Strategy 3: Skim for main ideas

Read first and last sentences of paragraphs for gist.

Strategy 4: Read actively

Underline, note questions, predict what comes next.

Strategy 5: Guess from context

Use surrounding words to understand unknown vocabulary.

Practice activities

  1. Preview one article — write 3 predictions
  2. Scan a text for 5 specific facts in 2 minutes
  3. Skim a 500-word article, write main idea in one sentence

Related pages:

  • Reading — full chapter
  • Graded Readings
  • Dari companion

Graded Readings

Learning goal: Read texts at your level to build skills

Reading levels

  • Level A: 50-100 words, present tense, common words
  • Level B: 100-200 words, simple past, basic descriptions
  • Level C: 200-400 words, opinions, multiple tenses
  • Level D: 400-800 words, arguments, complex sentences
  • Level E: 800+ words, academic/professional content

How to use graded readings

  1. Choose text where you know ~80% of words
  2. Read once without dictionary
  3. Read again, guess unknown words
  4. Check meanings of 3-5 key unknown words only
  5. Write 2-3 sentence summary

Sample texts available in appendices

See Graded Readings Appendix for full collection.

Related pages:

  • Reading — full chapter
  • Reading Strategies
  • Vocabulary

Writing Skills

Stage 1 → Stage 4 · 10–15 min daily

Learning goals

  1. Write correct simple sentences (SVO order)
  2. Compose chat-length and email-length messages
  3. Build paragraphs with topic sentence + details
  4. Use punctuation and capitalization
  5. Edit your own work with a simple checklist

Why this skill matters

Writing makes thinking clear. It helps you apply for courses, send professional messages, and express yourself when speaking is not possible.

Write messy first. Edit second. Courage first, perfection later.

Level focus

Stage 1: sentences · Stage 2: paragraphs · Stage 3+: formal writing

Step-by-step lessons

Lesson 1 — Sentence skeleton (15 min)

Subject + Verb + Object (American order)

PartExample
SubjectI
Verbstudy
ObjectEnglish
  • I study English. ✓
  • Study I English. ✗

Dari note: Dari allows flexible order. English is stricter — subject usually first.

Lesson 2 — Capitalization and punctuation (15 min)

Capitalize:

  • First word of sentence
  • I
  • Names, places: Fatima, Afghanistan, Monday
  • Language: English

Punctuation:

MarkUse
.end of statement
?question
!strong feeling (use rarely)
,short pause in list or clause

Example:

Hello, my name is Laila. I live in Afghanistan. Do you study English?

Lesson 3 — Chat writing (10 min)

Short, clear, polite:

Good morning! I am fine today. I studied English for 30 minutes. How are you?

Avoid: all caps (looks angry), too many !!!

Lesson 4 — Daily journal (15 min)

Template (5 sentences):

  1. Today I…
  2. I felt…
  3. I learned…
  4. Tomorrow I will…
  5. I am grateful for…

Optional: journal helps emotions — keep private.

Lesson 5 — Paragraph structure (20 min)

One paragraph = 5–8 sentences

  1. Topic sentence — main idea
  2. Detail — example or reason
  3. Detail — second example
  4. Closing — summary or feeling

Example topic: Why I study English

I study English because it gives me independence. I can take online courses and read global news. I practice thirty minutes every day at home on my phone. Some days are hard, but I continue. English is a door to my future, and I choose to open it.

Lesson 6 — Linking words (15 min)

TypeWords
Addand, also, moreover
Contrastbut, however, although
Reasonbecause, since
Orderfirst, then, finally
Examplefor example, such as

Lesson 7 — Self-editing checklist (10 min)

Before you finish:

  • Every sentence starts with capital
  • Subject + verb match (she goes, not she go)
  • Period at end
  • I is capital
  • Read aloud — does it sound right?

Examples

Stage 1 — 5 sentences about today:

I woke up early. I helped my family. I studied English on my phone. I learned ten new words. I feel proud tonight.

Stage 2 — email (informal):

Subject: Question about the online course

Dear Teacher,

My name is Hawa. I am interested in your free English course. Can you tell me how to register? Thank you for your time.

Best regards,
Hawa

Practice tasks

  1. Write today’s journal (5 sentences).
  2. Write one paragraph: My study routine.
  3. Edit yesterday’s writing with the checklist.
  4. Drills: exercises/09-writing-drills.md

Self-check questions

  1. What is SVO order?
  2. When do you capitalize letters?
  3. What is a topic sentence?
  4. Name three linking words for contrast and reason.
  5. What are five items on the editing checklist?

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
i instead of IAlways capital I
Run-on sentences (no period)Split into two sentences
Missing articlesAdd a/the where needed (03-core-grammar.md)
Dari word orderRead aloud — if odd, reorder

Milestone goals

WhenGoal
Week 25 correct sentences daily
Month 1One paragraph (80 words)
Month 3Email format correct
Month 6Essay 150+ words with linking words

Study habits

  • Write after vocabulary/grammar — use new words
  • Keep all journals in one note — see growth
  • Type on phone keyboard — real skill for messaging

Extended practice

4-week writing path:

WeekOutput
15 sentences daily
2Chat messages (3/day)
3One paragraph (100 words)
4Email draft (formal)

Peer check: Sister reads one paragraph — only clarity, not shame.

Unit quiz: exercises/unit-quiz-09-writing.md

Sentence Writing

Learning goal: Write clear, correct English sentences

Basic sentence structure (SVO)

Subject + Verb + Object

  • I study English.
  • She reads books.
  • We drink tea.

Types of sentences

  1. Statement: I am learning English.
  2. Question: Do you study daily?
  3. Command: Read this sentence.
  4. Exclamation: I passed the test!

Common sentence patterns

  • Subject + be + adjective: I am tired.
  • Subject + have + noun: She has a phone.
  • Subject + verb + object: We eat breakfast.
  • There + be + noun: There is hope.

Practice

Write 10 sentences about your day using different verbs.

Related: Writing | Grammar

Paragraph Writing

Learning goal: Organize sentences into clear paragraphs

Paragraph structure

5-8 sentences:

  1. Topic sentence (main idea)
  2. Supporting details (2-4 sentences)
  3. Example (optional)
  4. Concluding sentence

Topic sentence tips

  • States the main idea clearly
  • Usually first sentence
  • Specific enough to guide paragraph
  • Example: "Daily English practice improves skills faster than weekend marathons."

Linking words between sentences

  • Addition: also, moreover, furthermore
  • Example: for example, for instance
  • Contrast: however, on the other hand
  • Result: therefore, as a result

Practice

Write one paragraph (6-8 sentences) about why you study English.

Related: Writing | Sentences

Journal Practice

Learning goal: Build writing fluency through daily journaling

Why journal in English?

  • Practice without judgment
  • Track progress over time
  • Apply new vocabulary immediately
  • Build writing stamina
  • Process emotions in target language

Daily journal template (5-10 minutes)

  1. Date: Today is [date]
  2. Feeling: I feel [emotion] because...
  3. Did: Today I [action]
  4. Learned: I learned [new thing]
  5. Tomorrow: Tomorrow I will [plan]

Journal prompts

  • Describe your morning routine
  • Write about a challenge you overcame
  • Describe a family member
  • What are you grateful for?
  • What is your dream for next year?

Privacy & storage

  • Keep in password-protected notes app
  • Use code name if needed
  • Delete or archive older entries
  • Don't share unless you want to

Related: Writing | Study Strategies

Intermediate Growth (B1 → B2)

Stage 2 · Months 4–8

Learning goals

  1. Use past tense for stories and experiences
  2. Use future forms (will, going to, present continuous for plans)
  3. Express opinions and reasons
  4. Connect ideas with linking words
  5. Speak and write 2–5 minutes on familiar topics

Why this skill matters

Beginner English describes now. Intermediate English describes your life story and your plans — past hardships, future dreams, present choices.

You are no longer “studying English.” You are using English to live and think.

Level focus

Stage 2 (B1 → B2 bridge).

Prerequisite: Solid Stage 1 — present tense, 300+ words, basic listening.

Step-by-step lessons

Lesson 1 — Past simple: regular verbs (20 min)

Form: verb + -ed (walk → walked)

AffirmativeNegativeQuestion
I walkedI did not walkDid you walk?
She studiedShe did not studyDid she study?

Spelling rules:

  • stop → stopped (double consonant)
  • try → tried (y → ied)
  • play → played

Examples:

Yesterday I studied English for forty minutes.
Last year I started learning at home.
She helped her sister with homework.

Lesson 2 — Past simple: irregular verbs (25 min)

Learn in groups of 5 per week:

BasePast
gowent
seesaw
makemade
taketook
comecame
getgot
knowknew
thinkthought
saysaid
feelfelt
havehad
bewas / were

Story practice:

Last week I went online. I saw a free course. I took notes. I felt nervous but happy.

Lesson 3 — Past continuous (15 min)

Was/were + verb-ing — action in progress in the past

I was studying when my mother called.
They were watching a lesson at 8 p.m.

Contrast:

I studied (finished action) vs I was studying (in progress)

Lesson 4 — Future forms (20 min)

FormUseExample
willdecision, promise, predictionI will practice tomorrow.
going toplan already decidedI am going to finish Module 10.
present continuousfixed arrangementI am meeting my sister tonight to study.

Lesson 5 — Modals for ability and advice (20 min)

ModalMeaningExample
can / couldabilityI can read slow English.
shouldadviceYou should review old words.
must / have tonecessityI must rest when I am sick.
may / mightpossibilityIt might rain; I will study inside.

Lesson 6 — Opinions (15 min)

PhraseExample
I think…I think daily practice works.
In my opinion…In my opinion, women should have education.
I believe…I believe hope is important.
I agree / disagreeI disagree with stopping after one hard day.

Structure: Opinion + reason + example

I think English opens opportunities. Because many courses are online. For example, I found free grammar lessons.

Lesson 7 — Connectors and storytelling (20 min)

Timeline words: first, then, after that, later, finally

Contrast: however, although, but

Cause: because, since, so

Mini story (90 sec spoken):

First, I was afraid to speak. Then I recorded my voice every week. After that, I understood more listening clips. Finally, I spoke for two minutes without stopping. Although I still make mistakes, I continue.

Lesson 8 — Fluency bridges (15 min)

Fillers (natural, not too many):

  • Well, … / Let me see, … / I mean, …

Rephrase when stuck:

Sorry, let me say that another way. …
What I want to say is, …

Practice: 2-minute talk on a challenge I overcame.

Examples

Past + opinion paragraph:

Last month I had a difficult week. I did not study for three days because I was tired. However, I started again on Monday. I think consistency matters more than perfection. I am going to study thirty minutes daily this month. I believe small steps change lives.

Practice tasks

  1. Write 10 past tense sentences about last week.
  2. Write 5 future sentences (will + going to).
  3. Record 2-minute story with timeline connectors.
  4. Drills: exercises/10-intermediate-drills.md

Self-check questions

  1. How do you form past tense for regular verbs?
  2. Past of go, see, have?
  3. When use will vs going to?
  4. Write one opinion + because + example.
  5. Name four timeline connectors.

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
I go yesterdayI went yesterday
Did you went?Did you go?
I will goingI am going to go / I will go
No connector in long speechAdd first, then, finally

Milestone goals

WhenGoal
Month 4Past tense regular + 10 irregular
Month 5Future + modals in speech
Month 62-min story with connectors
Month 8B1 checkpoint quiz pass (14-assessments.md)

Study habits

  • Monday: past · Wednesday: future · Friday: opinion speaking
  • Re-use one life story — add detail each week (deeper, not always new)

Extended practice

Irregular verb bank (learn 5/week): go, see, make, take, come, get, know, think, say, feel, have, be, do, eat, drink, write, read, speak, buy, tell.

Story template: Last [time], I [past verb]. Then I [past verb]. Finally, I [past verb]. Now I [present]. Tomorrow I will [future].

Fluency: building-fluency.md — week 5–12 challenges.

Unit quiz: exercises/unit-quiz-10-intermediate.md

Past Tenses

Learning goal: Tell stories and describe past experiences

Past Simple

Regular verbs: Add -ed

  • walk → walked
  • study → studied
  • play → played

Irregular verbs (learn weekly):

  • go → went
  • see → saw
  • have → had
  • make → made
  • take → took

Use: Completed actions in the past

Yesterday I studied for two hours. Last week I went to the market.

Past Continuous

Was/were + verb-ing

Use: Actions in progress at specific past time

I was studying when my sister called.

Past Simple vs Past Continuous

  • Simple: I studied English yesterday. (finished action)
  • Continuous: I was studying when you arrived. (in progress)

Practice

  1. Write 10 sentences about last week using past simple
  2. Write 5 sentences using past continuous
  3. Combine: Write a story using both

Related: Intermediate Growth | Grammar

Future Forms

Learning goal: Express plans, predictions, and promises

Three main future forms

1. Will

Use: Decisions made now, promises, predictions

  • I will study tomorrow.
  • It will rain later.
  • I will help you.

2. Going to

Use: Plans already decided, predictions with evidence

  • I am going to finish this module today. (plan)
  • Look at those clouds — it's going to rain. (evidence)

3. Present Continuous for future

Use: Fixed arrangements

  • I am meeting my sister tonight to practice English.
  • We are having class at 6 PM.

Choosing the right form

SituationFormExample
Sudden decisionwillI'll help you right now!
Pre-plannedgoing toI'm going to study tonight.
Scheduled eventpresent continuousClass is starting at 9 AM.

Practice

Write 5 sentences each with will, going to, and present continuous for future.

Related: Intermediate Growth

Building Fluency

Learning goal: Speak and write smoothly without long pauses

What is fluency?

Fluency = smooth communication even with some mistakes

Not: Perfect grammar at slow speed
Yes: Clear communication at natural pace

Fluency-building techniques

1. Timed writing (10 min)

Write continuously for 10 minutes without stopping. Don't edit, just write.

2. 2-minute talks

Speak about one topic for 2 minutes without stopping. Record yourself.

3. Shadowing

Repeat after native speakers to match rhythm and flow.

4. Reduce self-correction

Let small mistakes go while speaking. Fix them later in writing.

5. Use chunks

Learn phrases as units: "as a matter of fact," "to be honest"

Fluency vs accuracy

  • Beginner: Focus on accuracy (correct sentences)
  • Intermediate: Balance both
  • Advanced: Fluency with monitoring

Weekly fluency challenges

  • Week 1: Write 150 words in 10 minutes daily
  • Week 2: Speak for 2 minutes on 5 different topics
  • Week 3: Shadow 5 minutes of audio daily
  • Week 4: Have one 10-minute conversation (with self, partner, or recording)

Related: Intermediate Growth | Speaking

Advanced Skills (B2 → C2)

Stage 3 → Stage 4 · Months 9–24+

Learning goals

  1. Build complex sentences (clauses, conditionals)
  2. Control register (formal vs informal)
  3. Write and speak professionally (email, presentations)
  4. Use hedging and nuance (maybe, tend to, it appears)
  5. Argue a position clearly and politely

Why this skill matters

Advanced English is not “more grammar rules.” It is precision, respect, and power — in school applications, remote work, advocacy, and global conversation.

You have something to say. Advanced English helps the world hear it clearly.

Level focus

Stage 3–4 (B2 → C1 → C2).

Step-by-step lessons

Lesson 1 — Relative clauses (20 min)

Who — people · which — things · that — both (informal)

The woman who teaches online inspires me.
The course which I took was free.
The book that I read was difficult.

Reduced: The woman teaching online… (advanced style)

Lesson 2 — Conditionals (25 min)

TypeStructureExample
ZeroIf + present, presentIf you heat ice, it melts.
FirstIf + present, willIf I practice, I will improve.
SecondIf + past, wouldIf I had time, I would study more.
ThirdIf + past perfect, would haveIf I had started earlier, I would have learned more.

Your life:

If I study daily, I will reach B2.
If I had fluent English now, I would apply for more scholarships.

Lesson 3 — Passive voice (15 min)

Be + past participle — focus on action/object

English is taught online.
The message was sent yesterday.
Mistakes are made — that is normal.

Lesson 4 — Register: formal vs informal (20 min)

InformalFormal
HiDear / Hello
Thanks a lotThank you for your assistance
I wantI would like
ASAPas soon as possible
kidschildren

Rule: Job, university, unknown adults → formal. Friends, sisters → informal.

Lesson 5 — Hedging and nuance (15 min)

Avoid sounding too absolute in academic/professional speech:

HedgeExample
may, mightThis may help learners.
tend toWomen tend to face more barriers.
it seems / appearsIt appears that practice works.
in many casesIn many cases, small groups help.

Lesson 6 — Argument structure (25 min)

  1. Claim — main point
  2. Evidence — fact or example
  3. Counter — other view (optional)
  4. Reply — why your claim still holds
  5. Conclusion

Topic: Education should be available to all women.

Claim: Every woman deserves access to education. Evidence: Studies show education improves health and income. Counter: Some say resources are limited. Reply: Long-term investment in women strengthens communities. Conclusion: Restrictions harm society; education is a right.

Lesson 7 — Professional email (20 min)

Structure:

  • Subject line (clear)
  • Greeting (Dear Ms. … / Dear Team)
  • Purpose (first paragraph)
  • Details (bullet points if needed)
  • Polite close (Thank you for your consideration)
  • Signature (name, optional contact)

Lesson 8 — Presentations (20 min)

Outline (5–7 min talk):

  1. Hook — question or short story
  2. Problem
  3. Your main points (3 bullets)
  4. Solution or hope
  5. Call to action — what listener can do
  6. Thank you

Practice: Record on phone; watch once; note filler words to reduce.

Examples

Formal sentence:

I am writing to inquire about the registration deadline for the online certificate program.

Nuanced opinion:

Although progress takes time, it seems that consistent self-study can lead to strong results, especially when learners face limited classroom access.

Practice tasks

  1. Write 5 sentences with who/which/that.
  2. Write 3 first conditional + 2 second conditional sentences about your goals.
  3. Draft one professional email (real or practice).
  4. Record 3-minute presentation outline on why I learn English.

Self-check questions

  1. When use who vs which?
  2. Give an example of first vs second conditional.
  3. What is hedging and why use it?
  4. Name five parts of an argument.
  5. What is different in formal email greeting?

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
Too informal in job emailUse Dear, would like, thank you
Too formal with close friendRelax tone
Every sentence passiveMix active + passive
No evidence in argumentsAdd one fact or personal example

Milestone goals

WhenGoal
Month 9Relative clauses in writing
Month 125-min structured presentation
Month 18Formal email without major errors
Month 24C1-style essay or long opinion piece

Study habits

  • Read one serious article weekly (news, UN, education blogs)
  • Copy one formal paragraph style per week
  • Debate with sister: 10 min English each side

Extended practice

Drills: exercises/11-advanced-drills.md

Portfolio (month 12+): Save 3 formal emails, 1 argument paragraph, 1 presentation outline in English folder.

Debate prompt (5 min write + 5 min speak): Online education helps women when classrooms are closed.

Unit quiz: exercises/unit-quiz-11-advanced.md

Complex Grammar

Learning goal: Use advanced structures correctly

Advanced grammar patterns

1. Relative clauses

  • The woman who teaches online is very helpful.
  • The book which I read was excellent.

2. Conditional sentences (all types)

  • Zero: If you heat water, it boils.
  • First: If I practice, I will improve.
  • Second: If I had time, I would study more.
  • Third: If I had started earlier, I would have progressed faster.

3. Passive voice

  • English is spoken worldwide.
  • The message was sent yesterday.

4. Reported speech

  • Direct: She said, "I am studying."
  • Reported: She said she was studying.

5. Subjunctive mood

  • I suggest that she study harder. (not "studies")
  • It's important that he be on time.

Practice

  1. Write 5 sentences with relative clauses
  2. Write one example of each conditional type
  3. Convert 5 active sentences to passive
  4. Report 5 things people said to you

Related: Advanced Skills

Professional English

Learning goal: Use English effectively in work contexts

Professional communication skills

Email writing

  • Subject: Clear and specific
  • Greeting: Dear Mr./Ms./Dr. [Last Name]
  • Purpose: State in first paragraph
  • Details: Bullet points if multiple items
  • Close: Thank you for your time. / Best regards,

Business vocabulary

  • meeting, deadline, proposal, project
  • collaborate, coordinate, implement
  • stakeholder, objective, deliverable

Professional phrases

  • "I would like to..." (instead of "I want")
  • "Could you please..." (polite request)
  • "Thank you for your consideration."
  • "I look forward to hearing from you."

Presentations

Structure:

  1. Introduction (purpose)
  2. Main points (2-3)
  3. Conclusion
  4. Q&A

Language:

  • "Today I will discuss..."
  • "Moving on to..."
  • "In conclusion..."
  • "Are there any questions?"

Professional meetings

  • Arrive/join on time
  • Introduce yourself clearly
  • Take turns speaking
  • Summarize action items at end

Related: Advanced Skills

Academic English

Learning goal: Read and write academic texts

Academic writing style

Formal language

  • Use third person: "Research shows..." (not "I think...")
  • Avoid contractions: "do not" (not "don't")
  • Use academic vocabulary: utilize, demonstrate, indicate

Structure

  • Introduction: Topic, thesis statement
  • Body paragraphs: Point, evidence, explanation
  • Conclusion: Summary, implications

Citations

  • Reference sources: According to Smith (2020)...
  • In-text citations and bibliography

Academic vocabulary

Verbs: analyze, evaluate, demonstrate, investigate, examine
Nouns: methodology, framework, hypothesis, conclusion
Connectors: furthermore, nevertheless, consequently

Critical reading

  1. Identify thesis and main arguments
  2. Evaluate evidence quality
  3. Note assumptions and biases
  4. Compare with other sources

Academic presentations

  • Clear thesis
  • Organized points
  • Evidence from sources
  • Formal delivery

Related: Advanced Skills

Study Strategies for Long-Term Success

All stages · Read early; revisit after breaks

Learning goals

  1. Build consistency without burnout
  2. Restart after gaps without shame
  3. Create a safe study environment
  4. Use motivation tools that work in hard times
  5. Study with a sister circle safely (optional)

Why this skill matters

Skills fade without habits. Strategies keep you learning when life interrupts school, internet, or confidence.

Discipline is remembering what you want. Courage is studying anyway.

Level focus

All stages — especially after breaks.

Step-by-step lessons

Lesson 1 — The 3 pillars (15 min)

PillarAction
TimeSame window most days (even 15 min)
MethodRotate skills (V/G/L/S/R/W)
MindsetKind self-talk

Missing one pillar? Fix the weakest — usually mindset after a hard week.

Lesson 2 — Motivation that lasts (20 min)

Weak motivation: “I must be fluent in 3 months.”
Strong motivation: “Today I will learn 10 words and use them.”

Write your why (keep private):

  • For my education
  • For remote work
  • To read books
  • To help my family
  • To prove I can

Read your why every Sunday.

Visual wins: checklist in 16-tracker.md — tick marks release dopamine.

Lesson 3 — Recovery after interruption (15 min)

If you stopped 1–7 days:

  1. Do not study double time to “catch up”
  2. Open tracker — read last win
  3. Study 15 minutes today only
  4. Return to normal plan tomorrow

If you stopped 1+ months:

  1. Re-take placement quiz (14-assessments.md)
  2. Review one module below your level (confidence)
  3. Start 12-week plan again at Week 1 rhythm — content may be faster second time

Shame is not a study method. Restart is.

Lesson 4 — Study space safety (15 min)

  • Chair or cushion, good light if possible
  • Phone on silent except study app
  • Water nearby
  • Tell one trusted person “I study now” if that protects your time
  • If interrupted often, use headphones + short sessions

Digital: separate folder for English; avoid mixing with stressful chats mid-session.

Lesson 5 — Sister study circle (optional, 20 min)

Safe circle rules:

  • 2–4 trusted women only
  • Meet by voice/chat at home — no unsafe travel required
  • Agenda: 30 min English only
  • Rotate: one teaches vocabulary, one asks questions
  • No criticism of accent — one correction per person per session
  • Share resources, not personal danger stories

Weekly format:

MinActivity
5Warm-up: each says one sentence
10Quiz each other on words
10Read one paragraph aloud
5Plan homework for alone days

Lesson 6 — Anti-burnout rules (10 min)

  • One day off per week is OK
  • Max 45 min if tired — quality over quantity
  • Switch skill if bored (grammar → listening)
  • Sleep and food matter for memory
  • Celebrate months studied, not only level jumps

Lesson 7 — Common learning myths (10 min)

MythTruth
“Adults cannot learn”Adults learn well with strategy
“Accent must be perfect”Clarity beats accent
“Grammar first, speak later”Speak small from day 1
“One app is enough”Rotate skills and sources
“Missing a day = failure”Habit returns with one session

Examples

Monday self-talk after a hard week:

I stopped for five days. That is human. Today I review ten words and listen to one clip. I am still a learner. I am still moving forward.

Sister circle message:

Sisters, tomorrow 4 p.m. we practice English 30 minutes. Bring three new words. We will read paragraph 2 from Reading module together.

Practice tasks

  1. Write your why in 2–3 sentences.
  2. Plan your safe study corner (draw or list).
  3. If you had a break, do 15-minute restart today.
  4. Optional: invite one trusted friend to 30-min English chat.

Self-check questions

  1. What are the 3 pillars?
  2. What should you do after a 2-week break?
  3. Name two sister-circle safety rules.
  4. Why is shame harmful to learning?
  5. What is a good goal for a tired day?

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
All grammar, no joyAdd favorite topic weekly
Studying 3 hours once, then zeroSmall daily beats rare marathons
Comparing to native children onlineCompare to your tracker
Isolating completelyOne safe partner helps accountability

Milestone goals

WhenGoal
Week 1Written why + study corner
Month 120+ study days logged
After any breakRestart within 48 hours
Month 6Optional circle running 4 weeks

Study habits (meta)

  • Sunday: read this file’s why again
  • Monthly: honest reflection in tracker
  • Yearly: re-read 01-overview.md — see how far you’ve come

Extended practice

90-day habit grid: Mark X for each study day — aim 70+ days in 90.

Confidence: building-confidence.md — read shame recovery script weekly.

Interruption plan: If you miss 7 days, do NOT study 3 hours once — do 15 min/day for 5 days instead.

Building Habits

Learning goal: Create sustainable English learning routines

The habit loop

  1. Cue: Time/place triggers study
  2. Routine: The study session itself
  3. Reward: Check mark, progress feeling

Start small

Don't begin with 2 hours daily. Begin with:

  • Week 1: 10 minutes daily (build consistency)
  • Week 2-4: 15-20 minutes (comfortable level)
  • Month 2+: 30-45 minutes (sustainable pace)

Habit stacking

Attach English to existing habit:

  • After breakfast → 10 words
  • While tea cools → listen to clip
  • Before bed → write 3 sentences

Track visually

  • Calendar with X marks
  • Tracker in Progress Tracker
  • Chain of days completed

Don't break the chain!

When habits break

Life interrupts. To restart:

  1. Do 5 minutes today (not zero)
  2. Return to normal schedule tomorrow
  3. No punishment, just continue

Related: Study Strategies

Dealing with Breaks

Learning goal: Restart learning after interruptions

Breaks are normal

Illness, family needs, stress, no internet — breaks happen.

Key truth: The break doesn't define you. The restart does.

After 1-7 days off

  1. Review last 10 words learned
  2. Do today's minimum (15 min)
  3. Resume full schedule tomorrow

Do NOT try to "catch up" with double sessions.

After 1-4 weeks off

  1. Review one easy module (builds confidence)
  2. Start at 15 min/day for 3 days
  3. Return to 30-45 min after that
  4. May need to drop back one level temporarily

After 1+ months off

  1. Retake placement quiz
  2. Review fundamentals (alphabet, common words if needed)
  3. Restart 12-week plan from beginning
  4. Will progress faster second time (not starting from zero)

Overcoming shame

"I stopped" = human
"I restarted" = brave

No apologies needed to yourself. Just open the book and begin.

Related: Study Strategies | Confidence

Maintaining Motivation

Learning goal: Stay motivated through challenges

Intrinsic vs extrinsic motivation

Extrinsic (external): job, certificate, approval
Intrinsic (internal): curiosity, growth, meaning

Both are valid. Intrinsic lasts longer.

Your "why"

Write down (keep private):

  • Why I learn English: [your reasons]
  • What it will help me do: [specific goals]
  • How I will feel when fluent: [emotions]

Read this monthly.

Motivation strategies

When bored

  • Change skill (grammar → listening)
  • Try new material (podcast, video)
  • Study favorite topic

When overwhelmed

  • Drop to 15-minute minimum
  • Review easy material (builds confidence)
  • Skip one skill temporarily

When lonely

  • Join online community (safe)
  • Study with sister/friend
  • Record voice messages to yourself

Small wins matter

Celebrate:

  • Spoke 1 sentence without stopping
  • Understood one full clip
  • Studied 7 days straight
  • Used new word in real life

Long-term vision

Where will you be in:

  • 6 months: [milestone]
  • 1 year: [milestone]
  • 2 years: [dream]

Related: Study Strategies

Overcoming Shame and Building Confidence

Learning goal: Study without shame after breaks or mistakes

Common shame sources

  1. "I stopped for [time period]"
  2. "My accent is bad"
  3. "I make so many mistakes"
  4. "Others are better/faster"
  5. "I should be fluent by now"

Truth reframes

Shame thoughtTruth
I stopped for 3 weeksI am restarting today. That is courage.
My accent is badMy accent shows I'm multilingual.
I make mistakesMistakes mean I'm practicing.
Others are fasterI compare only to past me.
Should be fluentLanguage takes years. I'm progressing.

Daily affirmations

Say aloud (or in your heart):

  • I am a capable learner
  • Small progress is still progress
  • My voice in English is valid
  • I restart as many times as needed
  • Education is my right

When criticism comes

External: If someone mocks your accent/level:

  • Their opinion doesn't define your worth
  • Leave the conversation if needed
  • Find supportive practice partners

Internal: If you criticize yourself:

  • Would you say this to a friend learning Dari? No? Then don't say it to yourself.
  • Note ONE thing done well today
  • Speak kindly: "I'm learning" not "I'm terrible"

Shame recovery script

When shame strikes:

  1. Acknowledge: "I feel ashamed because..."
  2. Truth: "But the truth is, learning takes time."
  3. Action: "Today I will do [small step]."
  4. Kindness: "I am doing my best."

Related: Study Strategies | Speaking Confidence

12-Week Beginner Plan

Learning goal: Complete beginner foundations (A1-A2)

Time: 30-45 min/day, 5-6 days/week

Week-by-week breakdown

Weeks 1-2: Foundations

  • Alphabet & phonics
  • First 100 words
  • Introduction dialogue
  • Present simple (I/you)

Weeks 3-4: Core grammar

  • Be & have
  • Present simple (all persons)
  • Questions & negatives
  • Articles (a, an, the)

Weeks 5-6: Expanding skills

  • Vocabulary to 200 words
  • Basic pronunciation (TH, V, W)
  • Listening to slow clips
  • Speaking: record introduction

Weeks 7-8: Building fluency

  • Vocabulary to 300 words
  • Reading short texts
  • Writing 5-sentence journal
  • Listening without subtitles (try)

Weeks 9-10: Past tense intro

  • Past simple regular verbs
  • 10 common irregular verbs
  • Story writing (past tense)
  • Describing yesterday

Weeks 11-12: Review & checkpoint

  • Review all grammar
  • Take unit quizzes
  • Checkpoint test
  • Plan next 12 weeks

Daily routine template

Monday: Vocabulary + pronunciation
Tuesday: Grammar + writing
Wednesday: Listening
Thursday: Reading + review
Friday: Speaking
Saturday: Exercises/drills
Sunday: Rest or light review

Success criteria

By week 12, you should:

  • Know 300+ words
  • Use present & past simple
  • Understand slow English clips
  • Speak 1-2 minutes about self
  • Read 150-word texts
  • Write 100-word paragraphs

Related: Practice Plans

24-Week Intermediate Plan

Learning goal: Reach solid B1 level

Prerequisite: Complete 12-week beginner plan or place into Week 13

Time: 30-45 min/day, 5-6 days/week

Weeks 13-18: Intermediate grammar

  • Past tenses mastery (simple, continuous)
  • Future forms (will, going to, present continuous)
  • Modal verbs (can, should, must, might)
  • Present perfect introduction
  • Vocabulary to 500 words

Weeks 19-24: Fluency & skills

  • Opinions with reasons
  • Paragraph writing (8-10 sentences)
  • Listening at normal speed
  • Speaking 3-5 minutes
  • Reading articles (300-500 words)
  • Vocabulary to 700 words

Daily routine (45 min)

Monday: Vocabulary + pronunciation
Tuesday: Grammar deep dive
Wednesday: Listening (longer clips)
Thursday: Reading + comprehension
Friday: Speaking practice (record)
Saturday: Writing + self-edit
Sunday: Review weak area

Success criteria

By week 24:

  • Use past, present, future correctly
  • Express opinions with reasons
  • Understand most daily conversations
  • Speak 5 minutes on familiar topics
  • Read news articles with dictionary
  • Write formal emails

Related: Practice Plans | Intermediate Growth

52-Week Mastery Path

Learning goal: Reach B2-C1 level in one year

Time: 30-60 min/day consistently

Quarter 1 (Weeks 1-13): Foundations

  • Complete beginner plan
  • A1 → A2 level
  • 300+ words, basic grammar

Quarter 2 (Weeks 14-26): Intermediate

  • Past, future, modals
  • B1 level
  • 500-700 words

Quarter 3 (Weeks 27-39): Upper intermediate

  • Complex sentences
  • Conditionals, passive voice
  • B2 level approaching
  • 700-1000 words

Quarter 4 (Weeks 40-52): Advanced preparation

  • Professional/academic English
  • Presentations, formal writing
  • B2+ or early C1
  • 1000+ words

Monthly milestones

Month 3: Basic conversations
Month 6: Describe experiences fluently
Month 9: Discuss abstract topics
Month 12: Professional competence

Adjustment points

Week 13, 26, 39: Take checkpoint quiz. Adjust pace if needed.

If ahead: Move to next level materials
If behind: Extend current quarter by 2-4 weeks

Related: Practice Plans

Assessments and Progress Checkpoints

All stages · Try first · Keys in answer-keys/

Learning goals

  1. Find your starting level (placement)
  2. Check progress at 4 checkpoints
  3. Score speaking and writing with rubrics
  4. Know when to move up a stage
  5. Use results to adjust your study plan

Why assessment matters

Tests are not punishment. They are a mirror — they show what to study next.

A low score today is data, not destiny.

Placement quiz (25 questions)

Instructions: 45 minutes max. No dictionary. No answer keys until finished.

Check answers: answer-keys/14-placement-quiz-KEYS.md

Part A — Foundations (1–8)

  1. Write the English alphabet in order (or recite and write).
  2. What is 14 in English words?
  3. Complete: She ___ a student. (am / is / are)
  4. Complete: They ___ English every day. (study / studies)
  5. Negative: He likes tea. → He ___ ___ tea.
  6. Question: You live in Kabul. → ___ you live in Kabul?
  7. Choose: There ___ two books. (is / are)
  8. Translate idea to English (write one sentence): I thank you.

Part B — Grammar & vocab (9–16)

  1. Past of go: ___
  2. Complete: I ___ studying when she called. (was / were / am)
  3. Future: I ___ visit tomorrow. (will / am will / wills)
  4. Modal: You ___ rest if you are sick. (should / must both OK — choose one)
  5. Opposite of hot: ___
  6. Write one sentence with because.
  7. Article: I need ___ pen. (a / an / the)
  8. Write 5 words on the topic study.

Part C — Intermediate (17–22)

  1. Past participle used with have: I have ___ (finish) → ___
  2. Second conditional: If I ___ (have) more time, I ___ (study) more.
  3. Relative clause: The woman ___ teaches online is kind. (who / which)
  4. Formal: Choose better: I want help OR I would like assistance
  5. Write 2 sentences: past + future about your English learning.
  6. Connector: Complete with one word: I was tired, ___ I continued.

Part D — Advanced (23–25)

  1. Passive: English ___ (teach) in many countries.
  2. Hedge: Rewrite softer: English is easy. → ___
  3. Write 3–4 sentences: opinion on why education matters for women.

Scoring guide

ScoreLevelStart module
0–8Stage 0–102-beginner-foundations.md
9–16Stage 1–203-core-grammar.md + 10-intermediate-growth.md when ready
17–22Stage 2–310-intermediate-growth.md
23–25Stage 3–411-advanced-skills.md

Checkpoint 1 — End of beginner (Month 3)

When: After study-plans/12-week-beginner.md

Sections:

  1. Write 8 present tense sentences about your routine.
  2. Listen to 1-min slow audio — write 3 sentences you understood.
  3. Record 60-sec introduction.
  4. Read short text in 08-reading.md — answer 3 questions.
  5. Vocabulary: write 20 words from memory on topic home.

Pass guide: Complete 4/5 sections with understandable English.

Drills: exercises/14-assessment-drills.md
Keys: answer-keys/14-assessment-drills-KEYS.md

Checkpoint 2 — Mid-intermediate (Month 6)

  1. Write a 120-word paragraph: A challenge I overcame (past tense).
  2. Use 5 linking words (because, however, first, then, finally).
  3. 2-min recorded story.
  4. Dictation: 5 sentences from listening module.
  5. Answer: What will you do next month for English? (future forms)

Pass guide: Paragraph mostly clear; past tense mostly correct.

Checkpoint 3 — Pre-advanced (Month 12)

  1. Write formal email (100+ words) — request information about a course.
  2. One second conditional sentence about your life.
  3. 3-min presentation recording — outline in 11-advanced-skills.md.
  4. Read news article — summarize in 5 sentences.
  5. Comprehension quiz in exercises file.

Checkpoint 4 — Advanced review (Month 18–24)

  1. Argument paragraph (150 words) with claim + evidence + counter.
  2. Passive voice: 3 sentences about education.
  3. Informal vs formal: rewrite one casual message formally.
  4. 5-min presentation on My vision for my future.
  5. Self-score all rubrics below — honest ratings.

Speaking rubric (self-score 1–5)

Criteria135
ClarityHard to understandMostly clearClear, easy
FluencyLong pausesSome pausesSmooth for level
GrammarMany errorsSome errorsFew errors for level
VocabularyVery basicAdequateRich for topic
ConfidenceWhisper / stopUnevenSteady voice

Goal: Average 3+ at beginner checkpoint; 4+ at advanced.

Writing rubric (self-score 1–5)

Criteria135
Task completionOff topicMostly on topicFully on topic
OrganizationNo clear orderSome structureClear paragraphs
GrammarBlocks meaningSome mistakesMinor mistakes
VocabularyRepetitiveSome varietyGood variety
MechanicsNo caps/periodsSome errorsFew errors

Listening rubric (self-score 1–5)

LevelDescription
1Only isolated words
2Main idea with repetition
3Main idea first listen, slow audio
4Details with one replay, normal speed short
5Podcast 10 min with notes

When to move up a stage

FromToEvidence
Stage 01Alphabet + 100 words + intro
Stage 12Checkpoint 1 pass + 300 words
Stage 23Checkpoint 2 pass + past/future stable
Stage 34Checkpoint 3 pass + formal writing
Stage 4maintainMedia, work, teaching others

Practice tasks

  1. Take placement quiz this week.
  2. Schedule Checkpoint 1 date in tracker.
  3. Score one old recording with speaking rubric.

Self-check questions

  1. Should you use answer keys before finishing?
  2. What score range starts intermediate module?
  3. How many checkpoints exist?
  4. What is a “pass” on Checkpoint 1?
  5. Why use rubrics if you have no teacher?

Skipping speaking/writing in checkpointsDo all sections

Self-Assessment Rubrics

Learning goal: Evaluate your own progress honestly

Speaking rubric (A1-C1)

LevelCan do
A1Introduce self, use simple phrases
A2Describe daily routine, simple past
B1Express opinions, tell stories
B2Discuss abstract topics, argue
C1Present formally, use nuance

Writing rubric

Rate yourself 1-5 on:

  • Grammar correctness
  • Vocabulary range
  • Organization/structure
  • Clarity of ideas

Listening rubric

  • Understand slow English: Yes/Partial/No
  • Understand natural speed: Yes/Partial/No
  • Catch details without replay: Yes/Partial/No

Use rubrics monthly

Track progress over time. Celebrate improvements!

Related: Assessments

Unit Quizzes

Learning goal: Test knowledge after each module

Quiz locations

Each module has an associated quiz in exercises/ folder:

  • Unit Quiz 02 - Beginner
  • Unit Quiz 03 - Grammar
  • And so on...

How to use quizzes

  1. Complete the module first
  2. Take quiz WITHOUT looking at notes
  3. Check answers in Answer Keys
  4. Review topics you missed
  5. Retake after 1 week if score < 70%

Quiz schedule

See Unit Quiz Schedule for recommended timing.

Related: Assessments | Answer Keys

Checkpoint Tests

Learning goal: Measure overall progress at key milestones

Checkpoint schedule

Week 12: Beginner checkpoint (A1-A2)
Week 24: Intermediate checkpoint (B1)
Week 39: Upper intermediate checkpoint (B2)
Week 52: Advanced checkpoint (B2+/C1)

Checkpoint components

  1. Grammar (20 questions)
  2. Vocabulary (30 questions)
  3. Listening (1 clip, 10 questions)
  4. Reading (1 passage, 10 questions)
  5. Writing (100-200 words)
  6. Speaking (2-3 min recording)

Passing criteria

70%+ on each section to move forward confidently

Below 70%? Review that skill area for 2 more weeks.

Full checkpoint tests

Available in:

  • Assessments main chapter
  • exercises/checkpoint-* files

Related: Assessments | Practice Plans

Free and Low-Cost Resources

American English · Smartphone-first · Updated for self-study

Learning goals

  1. Find trusted free apps and websites
  2. Build an offline study pack
  3. Choose safe American media
  4. Avoid scams and unsafe language-exchange situations
  5. Match resources to your stage

Why resources matter

Good tools save time. Bad tools waste data and hope. This list favors free, reputable, offline-friendly options.

One app downloaded well beats ten apps opened once.

Smartphone apps (free tiers)

AppBest forStageNotes
DuolingoHabits, basics0–2Short daily; not enough alone
DropsVocabulary0–2Visual words; 5 min/day
AnkiSpaced repetition1–4Make own decks; download shared “English” decks carefully
HelloTalk / TandemOptional chat2+Safety rules in 07-speaking.md
YouTubeListeningallDownload clips on Wi-Fi
Google TranslateQuick lookupallDo not rely for full sentences; check one word
Notes / KeepTracker, wordsallBuilt-in, offline

Websites (free)

SiteUse
BBC Learning EnglishShort lessons, slow audio (American also elsewhere)
VOA Learning EnglishAmerican, slow news
USA LearnsStructured courses
British Council (grammar pages)Clear grammar charts — note US spelling differences
Project GutenbergFree books (advanced)
Simple English WikipediaGraded articles

American listening (safe, educational)

SourceLevelTip
VOA Learning English1–3Daily 15-min news
TED-Ed (subtitles)2–4Educational only
Slow English podcasts (search “slow English”)1–2Download episodes
Children’s stories (LibriVox)2Clear narration

Subtitle rule: English subtitles ON → later OFF (06-listening.md).

Direct links (bookmark on phone)

ResourceURLUse

LibriVoxhttps://librivox.org/Free audiobooks
Purdue OWLhttps://owl.purdue.edu/owl/purdue_owl.htmlWriting help

YouTube (search name): VOA Learning English · Rachel's English (American sounds) · TED-Ed

Offline: YouTube app → Download clip when Wi-Fi is available.

Offline pack (download on Wi-Fi)

Create folder: English offline

  • 5 slow audio files (5–10 min each)
  • 1 PDF or saved page: word list from 04-vocabulary-building.md
  • This roadmap’s modules copied to phone storage
  • 2 exercise files + hide keys elsewhere
  • Tracker template from assets/tracker-template.md

Reuse same files all week — depth over novelty.

Pronunciation tools

ToolUse
YouTube: “American English th sound”Model mouth
Forvo (website)Native word pronunciation
Your voice memoBest free tool — compare daily

Reading materials by stage

StageMaterials
0–1Module texts, simple dialogues, labeled images
2VOA articles, graded readers (PDF)
3News in Easy English, blogs on education/health
4Full articles, essays, nonfiction chapters

Writing feedback without a teacher

  1. Checklist in 09-writing.md
  2. Grammarly Free (browser) — suggestions, not perfect
  3. Compare to model paragraphs in modules
  4. Sister circle peer review (12-study-strategies.md)
  5. Record → listen for grammar you know

Low-cost options (if budget allows later)

  • One grammar book (American edition) — used copy OK
  • Italki single lessons (choose verified tutors; safety rules apply)
  • Coursera / edX audit free courses in English

Not required for success with this roadmap.

Safety online

  • No paid “guaranteed fluent in 7 days” scams
  • Do not send money to unknown tutors on social media
  • Protect email and phone in apps
  • Report/block rude partners immediately
  • Prefer learning content over random chat with strangers

Dari / bilingual note

Full Dari translation of this roadmap is not included (per plan). Use Dari only to understand a concept once, then return to English practice. Bilingual subtitles sometimes help understanding — switch to English subtitles as soon as possible.

Practice tasks

  1. Install 1–2 apps max — not ten.
  2. Download 3 VOA clips offline.
  3. Save this repo’s next module to phone files.
  4. Bookmark VOA + one grammar site.

Self-check questions

  1. Why not use ten apps at once?
  2. What goes in an offline pack?
  3. Name two American slow-news sources.
  4. What is the subtitle progression?
  5. What safety rule applies to HelloTalk?

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
Only DuolingoAdd listening + speaking from roadmap
Random YouTube hoursCurated slow content
Translate whole paragraphs dailyEnglish output daily
Unsafe chats for “practice”Follow speaking safety module

Extended resource list

YouTube search (educational): VOA Learning English · slow English conversation · American English th sound

Bookmark set (max 5): VOA · one grammar site · dictionary · roadmap folder · offline downloads

Sister share: One English article per week — summarize together in English.

Progress Tracker

Track small wins. Numbers are proof that you are moving forward.

Learning goals

By using this tracker weekly, you will:

  1. Build a daily study habit (even 15 minutes counts)
  2. See which skills you practice most and least
  3. Set realistic monthly goals
  4. Recover motivation after breaks
  5. Prepare for checkpoint quizzes in 14-assessments.md

Why tracking matters

When life is hard, memory lies. You forget that you learned 200 words last month. A tracker shows the truth: you are growing.

What gets measured gets improved — but kindness matters more than perfection.

Level focus

All stages (0–4). Update checklists as you advance.

Step-by-step: set up your tracker (15 minutes)

Lesson 1 — Choose your tool

Pick one:

  • Phone Notes app (simplest)
  • Paper notebook (no battery needed)
  • Copy assets/tracker-template.md into Notes

Lesson 2 — Baseline (Day 1)

Write today’s date and answer:

QuestionYour answer
Can I read the English alphabet?yes / some / no
Can I say hello and thank you?yes / no
Minutes I can study per day15 / 30 / 45
My main reason to learn English_____________

Lesson 3 — Weekly rhythm

Every Sunday (or your chosen day):

  1. Count study days (goal: 5–7)
  2. Total minutes
  3. One skill to improve next week
  4. Copy a new week from the template

Lesson 4 — Monthly checkpoint (30 minutes)

On the last day of each month:

  1. Complete the Stage checklist below
  2. Record yourself speaking 60 seconds — compare to last month (optional)
  3. Take the quiz for your level in 14-assessments.md

Stage checklists

Stage 0 — Pre-beginner

  • I know the English alphabet
  • I can type my name on a phone keyboard
  • I know 50 survival words
  • I can say: Hello, Thank you, My name is…

Stage 1 — Beginner (A1–A2)

  • I use present tense for daily habits
  • I know 300+ common words
  • I can write a 5-sentence message about my day
  • I understand slow English (clear audio)
  • I studied 60+ days total (not necessarily in a row)

Stage 2 — Intermediate (B1)

  • I use past tense for stories
  • I know 800+ words
  • I can write one paragraph (80+ words)
  • I understand normal-speed short videos with some repetition
  • I speak 2 minutes without switching to Dari

Stage 3 — Upper intermediate (B2–C1)

  • I use conditionals and complex sentences
  • I read news or blog articles with a dictionary sometimes
  • I write formal emails
  • I understand podcasts with focus

Stage 4 — Advanced (C1–C2)

  • I argue a position politely in English
  • I write clear multi-paragraph essays
  • I adjust tone (formal vs friendly)
  • I learn new topics primarily through English

Skills scorecard (rate 1–5 each month)

SkillMonth 1Month 2Month 3Month 4
Vocabulary
Grammar
Pronunciation
Listening
Speaking
Reading
Writing
Confidence

1 = very weak · 3 = improving · 5 = strong for my level

Sample week (filled in)

Week of: March 3
Goal: 35 min/day average

DayMinSkillsWin
Mon40V, GLearned 12 new words
Tue35L, SUnderstood a slow podcast intro
Wed0—Family duties — rest
Thu45R, WWrote 6 sentences about my day
Fri30G, SRecorded 1 minute voice note
Sat40ReviewRetested old vocabulary
Sun20LightRead overview again — recommitted

Total: 210 minutes · Days: 6/7 · Next week focus: listening

Practice tasks

  1. Set up tracker today (Lesson 1).
  2. Log every study day for 7 days — even 15 minutes.
  3. Rate yourself on the skills scorecard.
  4. Complete Stage 0 or Stage 1 checklist honestly.

Self-check questions

  1. Why is tracking helpful when you feel “I learned nothing”?
  2. What is a good minimum on a busy day?
  3. How often should you do a monthly checkpoint?
  4. Should you delete old tracker pages? (Hint: keep them to see growth.)

Common mistakes

MistakeFix
Tracking only hours, not skillsNote V/G/L/S/R/W each day
Quitting after a zero-day weekLog “rest” and start again Monday
Comparing to fluent YouTubersCompare to your checklist last month
Perfect pagesMessy notes are fine

Milestone goals

WhenGoal
Week 15 days logged
Month 1Stage 0 complete OR 20 days studied
Month 3Stage 1 checklist half done
Month 6First intermediate quiz attempted

Study habits (routine card)

When: Same time daily if possible (morning or after chores)
Where: Safe, quiet corner
How long: 30–45 min, or 15 min minimum
After study: One sentence in tracker: “Today I won by…”
After break: Open tracker → read last win → study 15 min

Link to printable template

assets/tracker-template.md

Extended tracker tips

Monthly reflection (write in English):

  1. Biggest win this month?
  2. Weakest skill?
  3. Plan for next month?
  4. Spoke aloud 12+ days? (yes/no)
  5. Kind to self after breaks? (yes/no)

Exercise Overview

Learning goal: Understand how to use practice exercises

Exercise types

1. Module drills

Practice specific skills after each lesson:

  • 02-beginner-drills.md
  • 03-grammar-drills.md
  • etc.

2. Unit quizzes

Test knowledge after completing each module:

  • unit-quiz-02-beginner.md
  • unit-quiz-03-grammar.md
  • etc.

3. Checkpoint tests

Comprehensive tests at major milestones

How to use exercises

  1. After learning: Do drills immediately
  2. Next day: Review drill results
  3. End of module: Take unit quiz
  4. Milestone: Take checkpoint test

Answer keys

All answers in answer-keys/ folder. Check AFTER completing exercises.

Related: All exercise files | Answer Keys

Exercises: Beginner Foundations

Try first. Keys: answer-keys/02-beginner-drills-KEYS.md

A — Alphabet

  1. Write the five vowels: _______________
  2. What letter comes after M? ___
  3. Spell study: ___

B — Match word to meaning idea

WordMeaning (write a–e)
1. family
2. water
3. happy
4. please
5. thank you

a) polite request · b) liquid we drink · c) group of relatives · d) gratitude · e) good feeling

C — Numbers

Write in English words:

  1. 11 = ___
  2. 18 = ___
  3. 20 = ___

D — Complete the dialogue

  1. A: Hello. My name ___ Sara.
  2. B: Nice to ___ you.
  3. A: Thank you ___ your help.

E — Write (3 sentences)

  1. Introduce yourself: name, age (or “I am a young woman”), country.
  2. Write one polite request using please.
  3. Write: “I study English at home.”

Speaking (no key)

  1. Record your introduction (30 seconds).
  2. Say the alphabet aloud once without stopping.

Exercises: Core Grammar

Keys: answer-keys/03-grammar-drills-KEYS.md

A — Verb be

  1. I ___ tired.
  2. She ___ at home.
  3. They ___ students.
  4. ___ you fine? (question)
  5. He ___ not busy. (negative)

B — Have / has

  1. I ___ a smartphone. (have)
  2. She ___ two sisters. (have)
  3. They ___ not ___ time today. (negative)
  4. ___ you have Wi-Fi? (question)
  5. He ___ a good dictionary. (have)

C — Present simple

  1. I ___ (study) English.
  2. She ___ (study) English.
  3. They ___ (not / like) cold weather.
  4. ___ he live here? (question)
  5. We ___ (want) water.

D — There is / are

  1. There ___ a phone on the table.
  2. There ___ many books.
  3. ___ there a quiet room? (question)

E — Articles

  1. I need ___ pen.
  2. She ate ___ apple.
  3. ___ internet is slow today. (specific situation)

F — Word order

Fix:

  1. English study I.
  2. lives she Kabul in?

G — Write 6 sentences

  1. Daily routine: 3 with have/has, 3 with present simple.
  2. One question with Do you have…?

Exercises: Vocabulary Building

Keys: answer-keys/04-vocabulary-drills-KEYS.md

A — Match

| 1. improve | a) machine for calls/internet | | 2. patient | b) get better | | 3. phone | c) able to wait calmly | | 4. courage | d) bravery | | 5. review | e) study again |

B — Fill in (study theme)

  1. I ___ English every day. (verb)
  2. My ___ is to read books in English. (noun – goal)
  3. I use my ___ for online lessons. (noun)
  4. Do not give up — ___ again. (two words: try ___)
  5. I feel ___ when I learn ten words. (emotion)

C — Word family

  1. educate → education, ___ (person who educates)
  2. create → creative, ___ (thing that is created)

D — Write sentences

Use each word once: hope, practice, mistake, notebook, online

13–17. Five sentences.

E — Thematic set (health)

  1. Write 5 English words on health (any correct words).
  2. Use doctor and better in one sentence.
  3. Use rest in a sentence about study breaks.

Exercises: Pronunciation

Keys: answer-keys/05-pronunciation-drills-KEYS.md

Some items are “say aloud” — keys give target words only.

A — Minimal pairs (circle the word you hear / practice both)

  1. ship / sheep
  2. live / leave
  3. very / berry
  4. pat / fat
  5. west / vest

B — th practice

Write the word; then say aloud 3 times:

  1. thank
  2. this
  3. mother
  4. three
  5. think

C — Stress (mark stressed syllable with CAPS)

  1. computer → ___________
  2. patient → ___________
  3. education → ___________
  4. happy → ___________
  5. begin → ___________

D — Sentence rhythm

Read aloud with strong stress on content words:

  1. I want to study English at home.
  2. She lives with her family.

Speaking task (self-score 1–5, no key)

  1. Record 30 seconds: focus on th in thank you, this, mother.
  2. Which sound is hardest for you? Write one sentence in English.

Exercises: Listening

Keys: answer-keys/06-listening-drills-KEYS.md

A — Dictation (write what you hear)

If alone: read these sentences aloud slowly yourself, pause, write, then check keys.

  1. I study English at home every day.
  2. She wants to learn English for her future.
  3. There are two books on the table.
  4. Thank you for your help.
  5. They do not understand the lesson.

B — Main idea (after one slow clip of your choice)

  1. Title or topic of clip: _______________
  2. One sentence main idea: _______________
  3. Three words you heard: ___ / ___ / ___

C — Signal words

Choose: because, but, so

  1. I was tired, ___ I continued.
  2. I study ___ I want a better future.
  3. She practiced daily, ___ she improved.

D — Subtitle strategy

  1. When should you use English subtitles at the start?
  2. When should you try without subtitles?

Short answer in English.

E — Planning

  1. Name one clip you will download for offline use.
  2. How many times will you repeat the same clip this week? (number)

Exercises: Speaking

Keys: answer-keys/07-speaking-drills-KEYS.md — model answers for written parts only.

A — Shadowing (say 3 times each)

  1. I am learning English step by step.
  2. Today I feel hopeful about my future.
  3. I study at home because I want a better life.

B — Self-talk outline (write 6 prompts, then speak)

  1. I wake up…
  2. I drink / eat…
  3. I study…
  4. I help…
  5. I feel…
  6. Tonight I will…

C — Polite phrases (complete)

  1. I agree. Me ___.
  2. I see your point, ___ I think…
  3. Let me ___ … (buy time)

D — Record (self-rubric, no key)

  1. Record 60 seconds: introduction.
  2. Rate clarity 1–5: ___
  3. One sound to improve tomorrow: ___

E — Safety

  1. Where will you store recordings on your phone?
  2. Name one rule for language exchange apps.

Short English answers.

Exercises: Reading

Keys: answer-keys/08-reading-drills-KEYS.md

Read this text

A Quiet Morning

Nadia wakes up at six. She drinks tea and opens her phone. She studies English for thirty minutes in a quiet room. Her sister sometimes listens and smiles. Today Nadia learns ten new words. She feels tired but proud. She believes small steps are important.

A — Comprehension

  1. What time does Nadia wake up?
  2. How long does she study English?
  3. Who sometimes listens?
  4. How many new words today?
  5. How does she feel?

B — Inference

  1. Does Nadia study alone always? (yes/no + one word why)
  2. Is her study serious? (yes/no)

C — Scanning (use text in 08-reading module sample if needed)

Class starts March 12 at 9:00 a.m. Email learn@example.com. Fee: free.

  1. Date: ___
  2. Time: ___
  3. Cost: ___

D — Vocabulary from context

  1. proud probably means: happy about achievement / angry / sleepy
  2. quiet means: loud / with little noise / fast

E — Write

  1. Two sentences: main idea of A Quiet Morning.
  2. One sentence: your opinion (I think…).

Exercises: Writing

Keys: answer-keys/09-writing-drills-KEYS.md

A — Fix capitalization and punctuation

  1. hello my name is hawa
  2. i live in afghanistan do you study english
  3. she reads books every night

B — SVO order

Fix:

  1. English I study home at.
  2. tea she drinks morning the in.

C — Journal template

Write 5 sentences:

  1. Today I…
  2. I felt…
  3. I learned…
  4. Tomorrow I will…
  5. I am grateful for…

D — Paragraph

Topic: Why I study English at home (80+ words)

  1. Include topic sentence + 2 details + closing.

E — Linking words

  1. Write one sentence with because.
  2. Write one sentence with however or but.
  3. Write one sentence with first or finally.

F — Edit checklist

  1. List 3 errors you often make.
  2. Which checklist item will you check first next time?

Personal answers — sample in keys.

Exercises: Intermediate Growth

Keys: answer-keys/10-intermediate-drills-KEYS.md

A — Past tense

  1. go → ___
  2. see → ___
  3. study → ___
  4. have → ___
  5. She ___ (visit) her sister yesterday.

B — Past continuous

  1. I ___ (study) when the power went out.
  2. They ___ (watch) a lesson at 8 p.m.

C — Future

  1. I ___ practice tomorrow. (will)
  2. I am ___ visit my friend tonight. (going to)
  3. We ___ (meet) at 4 for study. (present continuous plan)

D — Modals

  1. You ___ rest when sick. (should)
  2. I ___ read slow English now. (can)
  3. It ___ rain; I will study inside. (might)

E — Opinion + reason

  1. Write 3 sentences: I think… + because + for example.

F — Story (80 words)

  1. Write a short story: last week + first/then/finally.

G — Fix

  1. I go yesterday to school.
  2. Did you went home?

Exercises: Advanced Skills

Keys: answer-keys/11-advanced-drills-KEYS.md

A — Relative clauses

  1. The course ___ I take is free. (which/that)
  2. The student ___ helps me is my sister. (who)
  3. Combine: This is a podcast. I listen to the podcast daily.

B — Conditionals

  1. If she studies daily, she ___ (improve) — will
  2. If I had Wi-Fi, I ___ (download) more lessons. — would
  3. If they had started young, they ___ (learn) faster. — would have learned

C — Passive

  1. Many lessons ___ (record) online. (record)
  2. My homework ___ (finish) last night. (finish)
  3. English ___ (speak) worldwide. (speak)

D — Register

  1. Informal → formal: Thanks a lot for your help!
  2. Formal → informal (friendly sister): I would like to inquire about your schedule.
  3. Which is better for job application: Hey or Dear Hiring Manager?

E — Hedging & argument

  1. Soften: Women must learn English.
  2. Write claim + because + example (3 sentences).
  3. Write one counter + reply (2 sentences).

F — Professional

  1. Email subject line for scholarship question.
  2. Presentation bullet: problem + solution (2 bullets).
  3. Write 2 hedging phrases you will use this week.

Speaking

  1. Record 3-min outline: education and hope.
  2. Self-score formal tone 1–5 in #16–17.

Answer Keys Guide

Learning goal: Use answer keys effectively for self-study

How to use answer keys properly

✅ DO:

  • Complete exercise FIRST
  • Check answers AFTER finishing
  • Review mistakes carefully
  • Note patterns in errors
  • Redo incorrect items

❌ DON'T:

  • Look while doing exercise
  • Just check final score without reviewing
  • Feel shame about mistakes
  • Skip redoing wrong answers

Answer key locations

  • answer-keys/02-beginner.md
  • answer-keys/03-grammar.md
  • answer-keys/04-vocabulary.md
  • ... and so on for each module

Learning from mistakes

When you get something wrong:

  1. Read the correct answer
  2. Understand WHY it's correct
  3. Find the rule/pattern
  4. Create 2 similar examples
  5. Review in 3 days

Честит код (Honesty code)

Self-study requires honesty. Only you know if you checked answers first. Be honest with yourself — it's the fastest path to learning.

Related: Assessments | Exercises

Answer Keys: Vocabulary Drills

A

1-b, 2-c, 3-a, 4-d, 5-e

B

  1. study / learn
  2. goal / dream
  3. phone
  4. try again
  5. proud / happy

C

  1. educator / teacher
  2. creation

D — Sample

  1. I have hope for my future.
  2. I practice every day.
  3. A mistake is normal.
  4. I write in my notebook.
  5. I take an online course.

E

  1. Any five: doctor, medicine, sick, well, rest, pain, health, body, etc.
  2. I feel better after I see the doctor.
  3. I need rest after study.

Answer Keys: Pronunciation Drills

A

Practice both; know the difference. No single circle answer.

B

6–10. Words as written; th between teeth.

C — Stress (examples)

  1. comPUter
  2. PAYtient
  3. eduCAtion
  4. HAPpy
  5. beGIN

D

Read with stress on: want, study, English, home; lives, family.

Speaking

18–19. Personal. Target sounds: /θ/ in thank, this, think, mother, three.

Answer Keys: Listening Drills

A — Dictation (exact)

  1. I study English at home every day.
  2. She wants to learn English for her future.
  3. There are two books on the table.
  4. Thank you for your help.
  5. They do not understand the lesson.

B

6–8. Personal (clip-dependent). Accept if main idea clear.

C

  1. but
  2. because
  3. so

D — Sample

  1. At the start, when level is hard — use English subtitles.
  2. When you understand ~60% without text; or month 4+ per module.

E

14–15. Personal. Recommended: repeat same clip 3 times per week.

Answer Keys: Speaking Drills

A–B

Say sentences as written; self-talk content personal.

C

  1. too
  2. but
  3. think

D

13–15. Personal rubric scores.

E — Sample

  1. Private folder / hidden album / delete after review.
  2. No personal address; block rude users; trusted partners only; see module 07.

Answer Keys: Reading Drills

A

  1. six / 6:00
  2. thirty minutes / 30 minutes
  3. her sister
  4. ten
  5. tired but proud (both)

  1. no — sister sometimes listens
  2. yes

  1. March 12
  2. 9:00 a.m.
  3. free

  1. happy about achievement
  2. with little noise

  1. Nadia studies English every morning and learns new words. She feels proud.
  2. I think her routine is strong. (any reasonable opinion)

Answer Keys: Writing Drills

A

  1. Hello. My name is Hawa.
  2. I live in Afghanistan. Do you study English?
  3. She reads books every night.

B

  1. I study English at home.
  2. She drinks tea in the morning.

C–D

6–11. Personal; check caps, periods, SVO, paragraph structure.

E — Sample

  1. I study because English opens doors.
  2. I was tired, but I continued. / However, I continued.
  3. First, I review words. Finally, I rest.

F

15–16. Personal. Sample 16: capitalization / subject-verb / articles.

  5. visited

  2. were watching

  2. can
  3. might

  1. I think daily practice works because repetition builds memory. For example, I review ten words each morning.

F

  1. Personal; must include past time words and first/then/finally.

G

  2. Did you go home?

A

    14–15. Personal; structure required

F

  1. Sample: Question regarding scholarship deadline
  2. Sample bullets: Problem: limited classroom access. Solution: daily self-study plan.
  3. may, it seems, tend to, might, in many cases

19–20. Personal rubric

Worksheet Overview

Learning goal: Use worksheets for focused practice

Available worksheets

Located in assets/worksheets/:

  • Alphabet practice
  • Grammar exercises (be/have, present tense)
  • Vocabulary logs
  • Phonics & blends
  • Listening dictation templates
  • Writing journal templates
  • Progress review sheets

How to use worksheets

  1. Print or copy: Write in notebook or type on phone
  2. Complete independently: No looking at answers first
  3. Self-check: Use answer keys after completion
  4. Repeat: Redo worksheets monthly to see progress

Digital alternative

Can't print? Create your own version in notes app following the template structure.

Related: Exercises | Practice Plans
